[P23-S00053] PDF pages [2]
Section IX describes a globally trained GTN system for recognizing handwriting entered in a pen computer.

[P23-S00054] PDF pages [2]
This problem is known as “on-line” handwriting recognition, since the machine must produce immediate feedback as the user writes.

[P23-S00055] PDF pages [2]
The core of the system is a Convolutional Neural Network.

[P23-S00056] PDF pages [2]
The results clearly demonstrate the advantages of training a recognizer at the word level, rather than training it on pre-segmented, hand-labeled, isolated characters.

[P23-S00057] PDF pages [2]
Section X describes a complete GTN-based system for reading handwritten and machine-printed bank checks.

[P23-S00058] PDF pages [2]
The core of the system is the Convolutional Neural Network called LeNet-5 described in Section II.

[P23-S00059] PDF pages [2]
This system is in commercial use in the NCR Corporation line of check recognition systems for the banking industry.

[P23-S00060] PDF pages [2]
It is reading millions of checks per month in several banks across the United States.

[P23-S00061] PDF pages [2]
There are several approaches to automatic machine learning, but one of the most successful approaches, popularized in recent years by the neural network community, can be called “numerical” or gradient-based learning.

[P23-S00062] PDF pages [2]
The learning machine computes a function Y?

[P23-S00063] PDF pages [2]
In a pattern recognition setting, the output Y?

[P23-S00064] PDF pages [2]
The average loss function Et,ain(W) is the average of the errors ?

[P23-S00065] PDF pages [2]
In the simplest setting, the learning problem consists in finding the value of W that minimizes Etyin(W).

[P23-S00066] PDF pages [2]
In practice, the performance of the system on a training set is of little interest.

[P23-S00067] PDF pages [2]
The more relevant measure is the error rate of the system in the field, where it would be used in practice.

[P23-S00068] PDF pages [2]
This performance is estimated by measuring the accuracy on a set of samples disjoint from the training set, called the test set.

[P23-S00069] PDF pages [3]
This gap always decreases when the number of training samples increases.

[P23-S00070] PDF pages [3]
Furthermore, as the capacity h increases, Eypain decreases.

[P23-S00071] PDF pages [3]
Therefore, when increasing the capacity h, there is a trade-off between the decrease of Eyrqin and the in-crease of the gap, with an optimal value of the capacity h that achieves the lowest generalization error E4.5¢.

[P23-S00072] PDF pages [3]
Most learning algorithms attempt to minimize Ey;ain as well as some estimate of the gap.

[P23-S00073] PDF pages [3]
A formal version of this is called structural risk minimization [6], [7], and is based on defining a sequence of learning machines of increasing capacity, corresponding to a sequence of subsets of the parameter space such that each subset is a superset of the previous subset.

[P23-S00074] PDF pages [3]
In practical terms, Structural Risk Minimization is implemented by minimizing Ey,ain + GH(W), where the function H(W) is called a regularization function, and ( is a constant.

[P23-S00075] PDF pages [3]
H(W) is chosen such that it takes large values On parameters W that belong to high-capacity subsets of the parameter space.

[P23-S00076] PDF pages [3]
Minimizing H(W) in effect limits the capacity of the accessible subset of the parameter space, thereby controlling the tradeoff between minimizing the training error and minimizing the expected gap between the training error and test error.

[P23-S00077] PDF pages [3]
The general problem of minimizing a function with re-spect to a set of parameters is at the root of many issues in computer science.

[P23-S00078] PDF pages [3]
Gradient-Based Learning draws on the fact that it is generally much easier to minimize a reasonably smooth, continuous function than a discrete (combinatorial) function.

[P23-S00079] PDF pages [3]
The loss function can be minimized by estimating the impact of small variations of the parameter values on the loss function.

[P23-S00080] PDF pages [3]
This is measured by the gradient of the loss function with respect to the parameters.

[P23-S00081] PDF pages [3]
Efficient learning algorithms can be devised when the gradient vector can be computed analytically (as opposed to numerically through perturbations).

[P23-S00082] PDF pages [3]
This is the basis of numerous gradient-based learning algorithms with continuous-valued parameters.

[P23-S00083] PDF pages [3]
In the procedures described in this article, the set of parameters W is a real-valued vector, with respect to which E(W) is continuous, as well as differentiable almost everywhere.

[P23-S00084] PDF pages [3]
In the simplest case, € is a scalar constant.

[P23-S00085] PDF pages [3]
More sophisticated procedures use variable ¢, or substitute it for a diagonal matrix, or substitute it for an estimate of the inverse Hessian matrix as in Newton or Quasi-Newton methods.

[P23-S00086] PDF pages [3]
The Conjugate Gradient method [8] can also be used.

[P23-S00087] PDF pages [3]
However, Appendix B shows that despite many claims to the contrary in the literature, the usefulness of these second-order methods to large learning machines is very limited.

[P23-S00088] PDF pages [3]
A popular minimization procedure is the stochastic gradient algorithm, also called the on-line update.

[P23-S00089] PDF pages [3]
It consists in updating the parameter vector using a noisy, or approximated, version of the average gradient.

[P23-S00090] PDF pages [3]
In the most common instance of it, W is updated on the basis of a single sample: Pk o* 7) (3) With this procedure the parameter vector fluctuates around an average trajectory, but usually converges considerably faster than regular gradient descent and second order methods on large training sets with redundant samples (such as those encountered in speech or character recognition).

[P23-S00091] PDF pages [3]
The reasons for this are explained in Appendix B.

[P23-S00092] PDF pages [3]
The properties of such algorithms applied to learning have been studied theoretically since the 1960's [9], [10], [11], but practical successes for non-trivial tasks did not occur until the mid eighties.

[P23-S00093] PDF pages [3]
Gradient-Based Learning procedures have been used since the late 1950's, but they were mostly limited to linear systems [1].

[P23-S00094] PDF pages [3]
The surprising usefulness of such simple gradient descent techniques for complex machine learning tasks was not widely realized until the following three events occurred.

[P23-S00095] PDF pages [3]
The first event was the realization that, despite early warnings to the contrary [12], the presence of local minima in the loss function does not seem to be a major problem in practice.

[P23-S00096] PDF pages [3]
This became apparent when it was noticed that local minima did not seem to be a major impediment to the success of early non-linear gradient-based Learning techniques such as Boltzmann machines [13], [14].

[P23-S00097] PDF pages [3]
The second event was the popularization by Rumelhart, Hinton and Williams [15] and others of a simple and efficient procedure, the back-propagation algorithm, to compute the gradient in a non-linear system composed of several layers of processing.

[P23-S00098] PDF pages [3]
The third event was the demonstration that the back-propagation procedure applied to multi-layer neural networks with sigmoidal units can solve complicated learning tasks.

[P23-S00099] PDF pages [3]
The basic idea of back-propagation is that gradients can be computed efficiently by propagation from the output to the input.

[P23-S00100] PDF pages [3]
This idea was described in the control theory literature of the early sixties [16], but its application to machine learning was not generally realized then.

[P23-S00101] PDF pages [3]
Interestingly, the early derivations of back-propagation in the context of neural network learning did not use gradients, but “virtual targets” for units in intermediate layers [17], [18], or minimal disturbance arguments [19].

[P23-S00102] PDF pages [3, 4]
The Lagrange formalism used in the control theory literature provides perhaps the best rigorous method for deriving back-propagation [20], and for deriving generalizations of back-propagation to recurrent networks [21], and networks of heterogeneous modules [22].

[P23-S00103] PDF pages [4]
A simple derivation for generic multi-layer systems is given in Section I-E.

[P23-S00104] PDF pages [4]
The fact that local minima do not seem to be a problem for multi-layer neural networks is somewhat of a theoretical mystery.

[P23-S00105] PDF pages [4]
It is conjectured that if the network is oversized for the task (as is usually the case in practice), the presence of “extra dimensions” in parameter space reduces the risk of unattainable regions.

[P23-S00106] PDF pages [4]
Back-propagation is by far the most widely used neural-network learning algorithm, and probably the most widely used learning algorithm of any form.

[P23-S00107] PDF pages [4]
D. Learning in Real Handwriting Recognition Systems Isolated handwritten character recognition has been extensively studied in the literature (see [23], [24] for reviews), and was one of the early successful applications of neural networks [25].

[P23-S00108] PDF pages [4]
Comparative experiments on recognition of individual handwritten digits are reported in Section III.

[P23-S00109] PDF pages [4]
They show that neural networks trained with Gradient- Based Learning perform better than all other methods tested here on the same data.

[P23-S00110] PDF pages [4]
The best neural networks, called Convolutional Networks, are designed to learn to extract relevant features directly from pixel images (see Section II).

[P23-S00111] PDF pages [4]
One of the most difficult problems in handwriting recognition, however, is not only to recognize individual characters, but also to separate out characters from their neighbors within the word or sentence, a process known as segmentation.

[P23-S00112] PDF pages [4]
The technique for doing this that has become the “standard” is called Heuristic Over-Segmentation.

[P23-S00113] PDF pages [4]
It consists in generating a large number of potential cuts between characters using heuristic image processing techniques, and subsequently selecting the best combination of cuts based on scores given for each candidate character by the recognizer.

[P23-S00114] PDF pages [4]
In such a model, the accuracy of the system depends upon the quality of the cuts generated by the heuristics, and on the ability of the recognizer to distinguish correctly segmented characters from pieces of characters, multiple characters, or otherwise incorrectly segmented characters.

[P23-S00115] PDF pages [4]
Training a recognizer to perform this task poses a major challenge because of the difficulty in creating a labeled database of incorrectly segmented characters.

[P23-S00116] PDF pages [4]
The simplest solution consists in running the images of character strings through the segmenter, and then manually labeling all the character hypotheses.

[P23-S00117] PDF pages [4]
Unfortunately, not only is this an extremely tedious and costly task, it is also difficult to do the labeling consistently.

[P23-S00118] PDF pages [4]
For example, should the right half of a cut up 4 be labeled as a 1 or as a non-character?

[P23-S00119] PDF pages [4]
The first solution, described in Section V consists in training the system at the level of whole strings of characters, rather than at the character level.

[P23-S00120] PDF pages [4]
The notion of Gradient-Based Learning can be used for this purpose.

[P23-S00121] PDF pages [4]
The system is trained to minimize an overall loss function which measures the probability of an erroneous answer.

[P23-S00122] PDF pages [4]
Section V explores various ways to ensure that the loss function is dif- ferentiable, and therefore lends itself to the use of Gradient- Based Learning methods.

[P23-S00123] PDF pages [4]
Section V introduces the use of directed acyclic graphs whose arcs carry numerical information as a way to represent the alternative hypotheses, and introduces the idea of GIN.

[P23-S00124] PDF pages [4]
The second solution described in Section VII is to eliminate segmentation altogether.

[P23-S00125] PDF pages [4]
The idea is to sweep the recognizer over every possible location on the input image, and to rely on the “character spotting” property of the recognizer, i.e. its ability to correctly recognize a well-centered character in its input field, even in the presence of other characters besides it, while rejecting images containing no centered characters [26], [27].

[P23-S00126] PDF pages [4]
The sequence of recognizer outputs obtained by sweeping the recognizer over the in-put is then fed to a Graph Transformer Network that takes linguistic constraints into account and finally extracts the most likely interpretation.

[P23-S00127] PDF pages [4]
This GTN is somewhat similar to Hidden Markov Models (HMM), which makes the approach reminiscent of the classical speech recognition [28], [29].

[P23-S00128] PDF pages [4]
While this technique would be quite expensive in the general case, the use of Convolutional Neural Networks makes it particularly attractive because it allows significant savings in computational cost.

[P23-S00129] PDF pages [4]
As stated earlier, most practical pattern recognition systems are composed of multiple modules.

[P23-S00130] PDF pages [4]
For example, a document recognition system is composed of a field locator, which extracts regions of interest, a field segmenter, which cuts the input image into images of candidate characters, a recognizer, which classifies and scores each candidate character, and a contextual post-processor, generally based on a stochastic grammar, which selects the best grammatically correct answer from the hypotheses generated by the recognizer.

[P23-S00131] PDF pages [4]
In most cases, the information carried from module to module is best represented as graphs with numerical in-formation attached to the arcs.

[P23-S00132] PDF pages [4]
For example, the output of the recognizer module can be represented as an acyclic graph where each arc contains the label and the score of a candidate character, and where each path represent a alternative interpretation of the input string.

[P23-S00133] PDF pages [4]
Typically, each module is manually optimized, or sometimes trained, outside of its context.

[P23-S00134] PDF pages [4]
For example, the character recognizer would be trained on labeled images of pre-segmented characters.

[P23-S00135] PDF pages [4]
Then the complete system is assembled, and a subset of the parameters of the modules is manually adjusted to maximize the overall performance.

[P23-S00136] PDF pages [4]
This last step is extremely tedious, time-consuming, and almost certainly suboptimal.

[P23-S00137] PDF pages [4]
A better alternative would be to somehow train the entire system so as to minimize a global error measure such as the probability of character misclassifications at the document level.

[P23-S00138] PDF pages [4]
Ideally, we would want to find a good minimum of this global loss function with respect to all the parameters in the system.

[P23-S00139] PDF pages [4]
If the loss function EF measuring the performance can be made differentiable with respect to the system's tunable parameters W, we can find a local minimum of E£ using Gradient-Based Learning.

[P23-S00140] PDF pages [4, 5]
However, at first glance, it appears that the sheer size and complexity of the system would make this intractable.

[P23-S00141] PDF pages [5]
To ensure that the global loss function E?

[P23-S00142] PDF pages [5]
(Z?, W) is differentiable, the overall system is built as a feed-forward network of differentiable modules.

[P23-S00143] PDF pages [5]
The function implemented by each module must be continuous and differentiable almost everywhere with respect to the internal parameters of the module (e.g. the weights of a Neural Net character recognizer in the case of a character recognition module), and with respect to the module's inputs.

[P23-S00144] PDF pages [5]
If this is the case, a simple generalization of the well-known back-propagation procedure can be used to efficiently compute the gradients of the loss function with respect to all the parameters in the system [22].

[P23-S00145] PDF pages [5]
For example, let us consider a system built as a cascade of modules, each of which implements a function X, = F,(Wn,Xn-1), where X,, is a vector representing the output of the module, W,, is the vector of tunable parameters in the module (a subset of W), and Xp~-1 is the module's input vector (as well as the previous module's output vector).

[P23-S00146] PDF pages [5]
The input Xo to the first module is the input pattern Z?.

[P23-S00147] PDF pages [5]
The Jacobian of a vector function is a matrix containing the partial derivatives of all the outputs with respect to all the inputs.

[P23-S00148] PDF pages [5]
The first equation computes some terms of the gradient of E?

[P23-S00149] PDF pages [5]
(W), while the second equation generates a backward recurrence, as in the well-known back-propagation procedure for neural networks.

[P23-S00150] PDF pages [5]
We can average the gradients over the training patterns to obtain the full gradient.

[P23-S00151] PDF pages [5]
It is interesting to note that in many instances there is no need to explicitly compute the Jacobian matrix.

[P23-S00152] PDF pages [5]
The above formula uses the product of the Jacobian with a vector of partial derivatives, and it is often easier to compute this product directly without computing the Jacobian beforehand.

[P23-S00153] PDF pages [5]
In By analogy with ordinary multi-layer neural networks, all but the last module are called hidden layers because their outputs are not observable from the outside.

[P23-S00154] PDF pages [5]
A completely rigorous derivation in more general cases can be done using Lagrange functions [20], [21], [22].

[P23-S00155] PDF pages [5]
Traditional multi-layer neural networks are a special case of the above where the state information X,, is represented with fixed-sized vectors, and where the modules are alternated layers of matrix multiplications (the weights) and component-wise sigmoid functions (the neurons).

[P23-S00156] PDF pages [5]
However, as stated earlier, the state information in complex recogni- tion system is best represented by graphs with numerical information attached to the arcs.

[P23-S00157] PDF pages [5]
In this case, each module, called a Graph Transformer, takes one or more graphs as input, and produces a graph as output.

[P23-S00158] PDF pages [5]
Networks of such modules are called Graph Transformer Networks (GTN).

[P23-S00159] PDF pages [5]
Sections IV, VI and VIII develop the concept of GTNs, and show that Gradient-Based Learning can be used to train all the parameters in all the modules so as to minimize a global loss function.

[P23-S00160] PDF pages [5]
It may seem paradoxical that gradients can be computed when the state information is represented by essentially discrete objects such as graphs, but that difficulty can be circumvented, as shown later.

[P23-S00161] PDF pages [5]
CONVOLUTIONAL NEURAL NETWORKS FOR ISOLATED CHARACTER RECOGNITION The ability of multi-layer networks trained with gradient descent to learn complex, high-dimensional, non-linear mappings from large collections of examples makes them obvious candidates for image recognition tasks.

[P23-S00162] PDF pages [5]
In the traditional model of pattern recognition, a hand-designed feature extractor gathers relevant information from the input and eliminates irrelevant variabilities.

[P23-S00163] PDF pages [5]
A trainable classifier then categorizes the resulting feature vectors into classes.

[P23-S00164] PDF pages [5]
In this scheme, standard, fully-connected multi-layer networks can be used as classifiers.

[P23-S00165] PDF pages [5]
A potentially more interesting scheme is to rely on as much as possible on learning in the feature extractor itself.

[P23-S00166] PDF pages [5]
In the case of character recognition, a network could be fed with almost raw in-puts (e.g. size-normalized images).

[P23-S00167] PDF pages [5]
While this can be done with an ordinary fully connected feed-forward network with some success for tasks such as character recognition, there are problems.

[P23-S00168] PDF pages [5]
Firstly, typical images are large, often with several hundred variables (pixels).

[P23-S00169] PDF pages [5]
A fully-connected first layer with, say one hundred hidden units in the first layer, would already contain several tens of thousands of weights.

[P23-S00170] PDF pages [5]
Such a large number of parameters increases the capacity of the system and therefore requires a larger training set.

[P23-S00171] PDF pages [5]
In addition, the memory requirement to store so many weights may rule out certain hardware implementations.

[P23-S00172] PDF pages [5]
But, the main deficiency of unstructured nets for image or speech applications is that they have no built-in invariance with respect to translations, or local distortions of the inputs.

[P23-S00173] PDF pages [5]
Before being sent to the fixed-size input layer of a neural net, character images, or other 2D or 1D signals, must be approximately size-normalized and centered in the input field.

[P23-S00174] PDF pages [5]
Unfortunately, no such preprocessing can be perfect: handwriting is often normalized at the word level, which can cause size, slant, and position variations for individual characters.

[P23-S00175] PDF pages [5]
This, combined with variability in writing style, will cause variations in the position of distinctive features in input objects.

[P23-S00176] PDF pages [5]
In principle, a fully-connected network of sufficient size could learn to produce outputs that are in-variant with respect to such variations.

[P23-S00177] PDF pages [5]
However, learning such a task would probably result in multiple units with similar weight patterns positioned at various locations in the input so as to detect distinctive features wherever they appear on the input.

[P23-S00178] PDF pages [5, 6]
Learning these weight configurations requires a very large number of training instances to cover the space of possible variations.

[P23-S00179] PDF pages [6]
In convolutional networks, described below, shift invariance is automatically obtained by forcing the replication of weight configurations across space.

[P23-S00180] PDF pages [6]
Secondly, a deficiency of fully-connected architectures is that the topology of the input is entirely ignored.

[P23-S00181] PDF pages [6]
The in-put variables can be presented in any (fixed) order without affecting the outcome of the training.

[P23-S00182] PDF pages [6]
On the contrary, images (or time-frequency representations of speech) have a strong 2D local structure: variables (or pixels) that are spatially or temporally nearby are highly correlated.

[P23-S00183] PDF pages [6]
Convolutional Networks force the extraction of local features by restricting the receptive fields of hidden units to be local.

[P23-S00184] PDF pages [6]
Convolutional Networks combine three architectural ideas to ensure some degree of shift, scale, and distortion invariance: local receptive fields, shared weights (or weight replication), and spatial or temporal sub-sampling.

[P23-S00185] PDF pages [6]
A typical convolutional network for recognizing characters, dubbed LeNet-5, is shown in figure 2.

[P23-S00186] PDF pages [6]
The input plane receives images of characters that are approximately sizenormalized and centered.

[P23-S00187] PDF pages [6]
Each unit in a layer receives in-puts from a set of units located in a small neighborhood in the previous layer.

[P23-S00188] PDF pages [6]
The idea of connecting units to local receptive fields on the input goes back to the Perceptron in the early 60s, and was almost simultaneous with Hubel and Wiesel's discovery of locally-sensitive, orientation-selective neurons in the cat's visual system [30].

[P23-S00189] PDF pages [6]
With local receptive fields, neurons can extract elementary visual features such as oriented edges, end-points, corners (or similar features in other signals such as speech spectrograms).

[P23-S00190] PDF pages [6]
These features are then combined by the subsequent layers in order to detect higher-order features.

[P23-S00191] PDF pages [6]
As stated earlier, distortions or shifts of the input can cause the position of salient features to vary.

[P23-S00192] PDF pages [6]
In addition, elementary feature detectors that are useful on one part of the image are likely to be useful across the entire image.

[P23-S00193] PDF pages [6]
This knowledge can be applied by forcing a set of units, whose receptive fields are located at different places on the image, to have identical weight vectors [32], [15], [34].

[P23-S00194] PDF pages [6]
Units in a layer are organized in planes within which all the units share the same set of weights.

[P23-S00195] PDF pages [6]
The set of outputs of the units in such a plane is called a feature map.

[P23-S00196] PDF pages [6]
Units in a feature map are all constrained to perform the same operation on different parts of the image.

[P23-S00197] PDF pages [6]
A complete convolutional layer is composed of several feature maps (with different weight vectors), so that multiple features can be extracted at each location.

[P23-S00198] PDF pages [6]
A concrete example of this is the first layer of LeNet-5 shown in Figure 2.

[P23-S00199] PDF pages [6]
Units in the first hidden layer of LeNet-5 are organized in 6 planes, each of which is a feature map.

[P23-S00200] PDF pages [6]
A unit in a feature map has 25 inputs connected to a 5 by 5 area in the input, called the receptive field of the unit.

[P23-S00201] PDF pages [6]
Each unit has 25 in-puts, and therefore 25 trainable coefficients plus a trainable bias.

[P23-S00202] PDF pages [6]
The receptive fields of contiguous units in a feature map are centered on correspondingly contiguous units in the previous layer.

[P23-S00203] PDF pages [6]
Therefore receptive fields of neighboring units overlap.

[P23-S00204] PDF pages [6]
For example, in the first hidden layer of LeNet-5, the receptive fields of horizontally contiguous units overlap by 4 columns and 5 rows.

[P23-S00205] PDF pages [6]
As stated earlier, all the units in a feature map share the same set of 25 weights and the same bias so they detect the same feature at all possible locations on the input.

[P23-S00206] PDF pages [6]
The other feature maps in the layer use different sets of weights and biases, thereby extracting different types of local features.

[P23-S00207] PDF pages [6]
In the case of LeNet-5, at each input location six different types of features are extracted by six units in identical locations in the six feature maps.

[P23-S00208] PDF pages [6]
A sequential implementation of a feature map would scan the input image with a single unit that has a local receptive field, and store the states of this unit at corresponding locations in the feature map.

[P23-S00209] PDF pages [6]
This operation is equivalent to a convolution, followed by an additive bias and squashing function, hence the name convolutional network.

[P23-S00210] PDF pages [6]
The kernel of the convolution is the set of connection weights used by the units in the feature map.

[P23-S00211] PDF pages [6]
An interesting property of convolutional layers is that if the input image is shifted, the feature map output will be shifted by the same amount, but will be left unchanged otherwise.

[P23-S00212] PDF pages [6]
This property is at the basis of the robustness of convolutional networks to shifts and distortions of the input.

[P23-S00213] PDF pages [6]
Once a feature has been detected, its exact location becomes less important.

[P23-S00214] PDF pages [6]
Only its approximate position relative to other features is relevant.

[P23-S00215] PDF pages [6]
For example, once we know that the input image contains the endpoint of a roughly horizontal segment in the upper left area, a corner in the upper right area, and the endpoint of a roughly vertical segment in the lower portion of the image, we can tell the input image is a 7.

[P23-S00216] PDF pages [6]
Not only is the precise position of each of those features irrelevant for identifying the pattern, it is potentially harmful because the positions are likely to vary for different instances of the character.

[P23-S00217] PDF pages [6]
A simple way to reduce the precision with which the position of distinctive features are encoded in a feature map is to reduce the spatial resolution of the feature map.

[P23-S00218] PDF pages [6]
This can be achieved with a so-called sub-sampling layers which performs a local averaging and a sub-sampling, reducing the resolution of the feature map, and reducing the sensitivity of the output to shifts and distortions.

[P23-S00219] PDF pages [6]
The second hidden layer of LeNet- 5 is a sub-sampling layer.

[P23-S00220] PDF pages [6]
This layer comprises six feature maps, one for each feature map in the previous layer.

[P23-S00221] PDF pages [6]
The receptive field of each unit is a 2 by 2 area in the previous layer's corresponding feature map.

[P23-S00222] PDF pages [6]
Each unit computes the average of its four inputs, multiplies it by a trainable coefficient, adds a trainable bias, and passes the result through a sigmoid function.

[P23-S00223] PDF pages [6]
Contiguous units have non-overlapping contiguous receptive fields.

[P23-S00224] PDF pages [7]
Architecture of LeNet-5, a Convolutional Neural Network, here for digits recognition.

[P23-S00225] PDF pages [7]
Each plane is a feature map, i.e. a set of units whose weights are constrained to be identical.

[P23-S00226] PDF pages [7]
The trainable coefficient and bias control the effect of the sigmoid non-linearity.

[P23-S00227] PDF pages [7]
If the coefficient is small, then the unit operates in a quasi-linear mode, and the sub-sampling layer merely blurs the input.

[P23-S00228] PDF pages [7]
Successive layers of convolutions and sub-sampling are typically alternated, resulting in a “bi-pyramid”: at each layer, the number of feature maps is increased as the spatial resolution is decreased.

[P23-S00229] PDF pages [7]
Each unit in the third hidden layer in figure 2 may have input connections from several feature maps in the previous layer.

[P23-S00230] PDF pages [7]
The convolution/sub-sampling combination, inspired by Hubel and Wiesel's notions of “simple” and “complex” cells, was implemented in Fukushima's Neocognitron [32], though no globally supervised learning procedure such as back-propagation was available then.

[P23-S00231] PDF pages [7]
A large degree of invariance to geometric transformations of the input can be achieved with this progressive reduction of spatial resolution compensated by a progressive increase of the richness of the representation (the number of feature maps).

[P23-S00232] PDF pages [7]
Since all the weights are learned with back-propagation, convolutional networks can be seen as synthesizing their own feature extractor.

[P23-S00233] PDF pages [7]
The weight sharing technique has the interesting side effect of reducing the number of free parameters, thereby reducing the “capacity” of the machine and reducing the gap between test error and training error [34].

[P23-S00234] PDF pages [7]
The network in figure 2 contains 340,908 connections, but only 60,000 trainable free parameters because of the weight sharing.

[P23-S00235] PDF pages [7]
Fixed-size convolutional networks that share weights along a single temporal dimension are known as Time-Delay Neural Networks (TDNNs).

[P23-S00236] PDF pages [7]
This section describes in more detail the architecture of LeNet-5, the Convolutional Neural Network used in the experiments.

[P23-S00237] PDF pages [7]
LeNet-5 comprises 7 layers, not counting the input, all of which contain trainable parameters (weights).

[P23-S00238] PDF pages [7]
The input is a 32x32 pixel image.

[P23-S00239] PDF pages [7]
This is significantly larger than the largest character in the database (at most 20x20 pixels centered in a 28x28 field).

[P23-S00240] PDF pages [7]
The reason is that it is desirable that potential distinctive features such as stroke end-points or corner can appear in the center of the receptive field of the highest-level feature detectors.

[P23-S00241] PDF pages [7]
In LeNet-5 the set of centers of the receptive fields of the last convolutional layer (C3, see below) form a 20x20 area in the center of the 32x32 input.

[P23-S00242] PDF pages [7]
The values of the input pixels are normalized so that the background level (white) corresponds to a value of -0.1 and the foreground (black) corresponds to 1.175.

[P23-S00243] PDF pages [7]
This makes the mean input roughly 0, and the variance roughly 1 which accelerates learning [46].

[P23-S00244] PDF pages [7]
In the following, convolutional layers are labeled Cx, subsampling layers are labeled Sx, and fully-connected layers are labeled Fx, where x is the layer index.

[P23-S00245] PDF pages [7]
Layer C1 is a convolutional layer with 6 feature maps.

[P23-S00246] PDF pages [7]
Each unit in each feature map is connected to a 5x5 neighborhood in the input.

[P23-S00247] PDF pages [7]
The size of the feature maps is 28x28 which prevents connection from the input from falling off the boundary.

[P23-S00248] PDF pages [7]
Layer S2 is a sub-sampling layer with 6 feature maps of size 14x14.

[P23-S00249] PDF pages [7]
Each unit in each feature map is connected to a 2x2 neighborhood in the corresponding feature map in C1.

[P23-S00250] PDF pages [7]
The four inputs to a unit in 52 are added, then multiplied by a trainable coefficient, and added to a trainable bias.

[P23-S00251] PDF pages [7]
The result is passed through a sigmoidal function.

[P23-S00252] PDF pages [7]
The 2x2 receptive fields are non-overlapping, therefore feature maps in $2 have half the number of rows and column as feature maps in C1.

[P23-S00253] PDF pages [7]
Layer S2 has 12 trainable parameters and 5,880 connections.

[P23-S00254] PDF pages [7]
Layer C3 is a convolutional layer with 16 feature maps.

[P23-S00255] PDF pages [7]
Each unit in each feature map is connected to several 5x5 neighborhoods at identical locations in a subset of 52's feature maps.

[P23-S00256] PDF pages [8]
Why not connect every $2 feature map to every C3 feature map?

[P23-S00257] PDF pages [8]
First, a non-complete connection scheme keeps the number of connections within reasonable bounds.

[P23-S00258] PDF pages [8]
More importantly, it forces a break of symmetry in the network.

[P23-S00259] PDF pages [8]
Different feature maps are forced to extract different (hopefully complementary) features because they get different sets of inputs.

[P23-S00260] PDF pages [8]
The rationale behind the connection scheme in table I is the following.

[P23-S00261] PDF pages [8]
The first six C3 feature maps take inputs from every contiguous subsets of three feature maps in $2.

[P23-S00262] PDF pages [8]
The next six take input from every contiguous subset of four.

[P23-S00263] PDF pages [8]
The next three take input from some discontinuous subsets of four.

[P23-S00264] PDF pages [8]
Finally the last one takes input from all $2 feature maps.

[P23-S00265] PDF pages [8]
Layer C3 has 1,516 trainable parameters and 151,600 connections.

[P23-S00266] PDF pages [8]
Layer S4 is a sub-sampling layer with 16 feature maps of size 5x5.

[P23-S00267] PDF pages [8]
Each unit in each feature map is connected to a 2x2 neighborhood in the corresponding feature map in C3, in a similar way as Cl and $2.

[P23-S00268] PDF pages [8]
Layer S4 has 32 trainable parameters and 2,000 connections.

[P23-S00269] PDF pages [8]
Layer C5 is a convolutional layer with 120 feature maps.

[P23-S00270] PDF pages [8]
Each unit is connected to a 5x5 neighborhood on all 16 of S4's feature maps.

[P23-S00271] PDF pages [8]
Here, because the size of S4 is also 5x5, the size of C5's feature maps is 1x1: this amounts to a full connection between $4 and C5.

[P23-S00272] PDF pages [8]
C5 is labeled as a convolutional layer, instead of a fully-connected layer, because if LeNet-5 input were made bigger with everything else kept constant, the feature map dimension would be larger than 1x1.

[P23-S00273] PDF pages [8]
This process of dynamically increasing the size of a convolutional network is described in the section Section VII.

[P23-S00274] PDF pages [8]
Layer F6, contains 84 units (the reason for this number comes from the design of the output layer, explained below) and is fully connected to C5.

[P23-S00275] PDF pages [8]
As in classical neural networks, units in layers up to F6 compute a dot product between their input vector and their weight vector, to which a bias is added.

[P23-S00276] PDF pages [8]
The function f is odd, with horizontal asymptotes at +A and -A.

[P23-S00277] PDF pages [8]
The constant A is chosen to be 1.7159.

[P23-S00278] PDF pages [8]
The rationale for this choice of a squashing function is given in Appendix A.

[P23-S00279] PDF pages [8]
Finally, the output layer is composed of Euclidean Radial Basis Function units (RBF), one for each class, with 84 inputs each.

[P23-S00280] PDF pages [8]
The outputs of each RBF unit y; is computed as follows: yi = D(a; - wis)'.

[P23-S00281] PDF pages [8]
The further away is the input from the parameter vector, the larger is the RBF output.

[P23-S00282] PDF pages [8]
The output of a particular RBF can be interpreted as a penalty term measuring the fit between the input pattern and a model of the class associated with the RBF.

[P23-S00283] PDF pages [8]
In probabilistic terms, the RBF output can be interpreted as the unnormalized negative log-likelihood of a Gaussian distribution in the space of configurations of layer F6.

[P23-S00284] PDF pages [8]
Given an input pattern, the loss function should be designed so as to get the configuration of F6 as close as possible to the parameter vector of the RBF that corresponds to the pattern's desired class.

[P23-S00285] PDF pages [8]
The parameter vectors of these units were chosen by hand and kept fixed (at least initially).

[P23-S00286] PDF pages [8]
The components of those parameters vectors were set to -1 or +1.

[P23-S00287] PDF pages [8]
While they could have been chosen at random with equal probabilities for -1 and +1, or even chosen to form an error correcting code as suggested by [47], they were instead designed to represent a stylized image of the corresponding character class drawn on a 7x12 bitmap (hence the number 84).

[P23-S00288] PDF pages [8]
Such a representation is not particularly useful for recognizing isolated digits, but it is quite useful for recognizing strings of characters taken from the full printable ASCII set.

[P23-S00289] PDF pages [8]
The rationale is that characters that are similar, and therefore confusable, such as uppercase O, lowercase O, and zero, or lowercase 1, digit 1, square brackets, and uppercase I, will have similar output codes.

[P23-S00290] PDF pages [8]
This is particularly useful if the system is combined with a linguistic post-processor that can correct such confusions.

[P23-S00291] PDF pages [8]
Because the codes for confusable classes are similar, the output of the corresponding RBFs for an ambiguous character will be similar, and the post-processor will be able to pick the appropriate interpretation.

[P23-S00292] PDF pages [8]
Another reason for using such distributed codes, rather than the more common “1 of N” code (also called place code, or grand-mother cell code) for the outputs is that non distributed codes tend to behave badly when the number of classes is larger than a few dozens.

[P23-S00293] PDF pages [8]
The reason is that output units in a non-distributed code must be off most of the time.

[P23-S00294] PDF pages [8]
This is quite difficult to achieve with sigmoid units.

[P23-S00295] PDF pages [8]
Yet another reason is that the classifiers are often used to not only recognize characters, but also to re-ject non-characters.

[P23-S00296] PDF pages [9]
Initial parameters of the output RBFs for recognizing the full ASCII set.

[P23-S00297] PDF pages [9]
The parameter vectors of the RBFs play the role of target vectors for layer F6.

[P23-S00298] PDF pages [9]
It is worth pointing out that the components of those vectors are +1 or -1, which is well within the range of the sigmoid of F'6, and therefore prevents those sigmoids from getting saturated.

[P23-S00299] PDF pages [9]
In fact, +1 and -1 are the points of maximum curvature of the sigmoids.

[P23-S00300] PDF pages [9]
This forces the F'6 units to operate in their maximally non-linear range.

[P23-S00301] PDF pages [9]
Saturation of the sigmoids must be avoided because it is known to lead to slow convergence and ill-conditioning of the loss function.

[P23-S00302] PDF pages [9]
The simplest output loss function that can be used with the above network is the Maximum Likelihood Estimation criterion (MLE), which in our case is equivalent to the Minimum Mean Squared Error (MSE).

[P23-S00303] PDF pages [9]
While this cost function is appropriate for most cases, it lacks three important properties.

[P23-S00304] PDF pages [9]
First, if we allow the parameters of the RBF to adapt, E(W) has a trivial, but totally unacceptable, solution.

[P23-S00305] PDF pages [9]
In this solution, all the RBF parameter vectors are equal, and the state of F6 is constant and equal to that parameter vector.

[P23-S00306] PDF pages [9]
In this case the network happily ignores the input, and all the RBF outputs are equal to zero.

[P23-S00307] PDF pages [9]
This collapsing phenomenon does not occur if the RBF weights are not allowed to adapt.

[P23-S00308] PDF pages [9]
The second problem is that there is no competition between the classes.

[P23-S00309] PDF pages [9]
Such a competition can be obtained by using a more discriminative training criterion, dubbed the MAP (maximum a posteriori) criterion, similar to Maximum Mutual Information criterion sometimes used to train HMMs [48], [49], [50].

[P23-S00310] PDF pages [9]
It corresponds to maximizing the posterior probability of the correct class Dp (or minimizing the logarithm of the probability of the correct class), given that the input image can come from one of the classes or from a background “rubbish” class label.

[P23-S00311] PDF pages [9]
In terms of penalties, it means that in addition to pushing down the penalty of the correct class like the MSE criterion, this criterion also pulls up the penalties of the incorrect classes: Pp EW)=5 Yo (ype (Z?, W) + log(e~F + Soe (7-))) p= a =1 (9) The negative of the second term plays a “competitive” role.

[P23-S00312] PDF pages [9]
It is necessarily smaller than (or equal to) the first term, therefore this loss function is positive.

[P23-S00313] PDF pages [9]
The constant 7 is positive, and prevents the penalties of classes that are already very large from being pushed further up.

[P23-S00314] PDF pages [9]
The posterior probability of this rubbish class label would be the ratio of e~J and e~J + 7, e7¥(4".W)_ This discriminative criterion prevents the previously mentioned “collapsing effect” when the RBF parameters are learned because it keeps the RBF centers apart from each other.

[P23-S00315] PDF pages [9]
In Section VI, we present a generalization of this criterion for systems that learn to classify multiple objects in the input (e.g., characters in words or in documents).

[P23-S00316] PDF pages [9]
Computing the gradient of the loss function with respect to all the weights in all the layers of the convolutional network is done with back-propagation.

[P23-S00317] PDF pages [9]
The standard algorithm must be slightly modified to take account of the weight sharing.

[P23-S00318] PDF pages [9]
An easy way to implement it is to first compute the partial derivatives of the loss function with respect to each connection, as if the network were a conventional multi-layer network without weight sharing.

[P23-S00319] PDF pages [9]
Then the partial derivatives of all the connections that share a same parameter are added to form the derivative with respect to that parameter.

[P23-S00320] PDF pages [9]
Such a large architecture can be trained very efficiently, but doing so requires the use of a few techniques that are described in the appendix.

[P23-S00321] PDF pages [9]
Section A of the appendix describes details such as the particular sigmoid used, and the weight initialization.

[P23-S00322] PDF pages [9]
Section B and C describe the minimization procedure used, which is a stochastic version of a diagonal approximation to the Levenberg-Marquardt procedure.

[P23-S00323] PDF pages [9]
RESULTS AND COMPARISON WITH OTHER METHODS While recognizing individual digits is only one of many problems involved in designing a practical recognition system, it is an excellent benchmark for comparing shape recognition methods.

[P23-S00324] PDF pages [9]
Though many existing method combine a hand-crafted feature extractor and a trainable classifier, this study concentrates on adaptive methods that operate directly on size-normalized images.

[P23-S00325] PDF pages [9]
Database: the Modified NIST set The database used to train and test the systems described in this paper was constructed from the NIST's Special Database 3 and Special Database 1 containing binary images of handwritten digits.

[P23-S00326] PDF pages [9]
NIST originally designated SD-3 as their training set and SD-1 as their test set.

[P23-S00327] PDF pages [9]
However, SD-3 is much cleaner and easier to recognize than SD- 1.

[P23-S00328] PDF pages [9, 10]
The reason for this can be found on the fact that SD-3 was collected among Census Bureau employees, while SD-1 was collected among high-school students.

[P23-S00329] PDF pages [10]
Drawing sensible conclusions from learning experiments requires that the result be independent of the choice of training set and test among the complete set of samples.

[P23-S00330] PDF pages [10]
Therefore it was necessary to build a new database by mixing NIST's datasets.

[P23-S00331] PDF pages [10]
SD-1 contains 58,527 digit images written by 500 different writers.

[P23-S00332] PDF pages [10]
In contrast to SD-3, where blocks of data from each writer appeared in sequence, the data in SD-1 is scrambled.

[P23-S00333] PDF pages [10]
Writer identities for SD-1 are available and we used this information to unscramble the writers.

[P23-S00334] PDF pages [10]
We then split SD-1 in two: characters written by the first 250 writers went into our new training set.

[P23-S00335] PDF pages [10]
The remaining 250 writers were placed in our test set.

[P23-S00336] PDF pages [10]
Thus we had two sets with nearly 30,000 examples each.

[P23-S00337] PDF pages [10]
The new training set was completed with enough examples from SD-3, starting at pattern # 0, to make a full set of 60,000 training patterns.

[P23-S00338] PDF pages [10]
Similarly, the new test set was completed with SD-3 examples starting at pattern # 35,000 to make a full set with 60,000 test patterns.

[P23-S00339] PDF pages [10]
In the experiments described here, we only used a subset of 10,000 test images (5,000 from SD-1 and 5,000 from SD-3), but we used the full 60,000 training samples.

[P23-S00340] PDF pages [10]
The resulting database was called the Modified NIST, or MNIST, dataset.

[P23-S00341] PDF pages [10]
The original black and white (bilevel) images were size normalized to fit in a 20x20 pixel box while preserving their aspect ratio.

[P23-S00342] PDF pages [10]
The resulting images contain grey levels as result of the anti-aliasing (image interpolation) technique used by the normalization algorithm.

[P23-S00343] PDF pages [10]
Three versions of the database were used.

[P23-S00344] PDF pages [10]
In the first version, the images were centered in a 28x28 image by computing the center of mass of the pixels, and translating the image so as to position this point at the center of the 28x28 field.

[P23-S00345] PDF pages [10]
In some instances, this 28x28 field was extended to 32x32 with background pixels.

[P23-S00346] PDF pages [10]
This version of the database will be referred to as the regular database.

[P23-S00347] PDF pages [10]
In the second version of the database, the character images were deslanted and cropped down to 20x20 pixels images.

[P23-S00348] PDF pages [10]
The deslanting computes the second moments of in-ertia of the pixels (counting a foreground pixel as 1 and a background pixel as 0), and shears the image by horizontally shifting the lines so that the principal axis is vertical.

[P23-S00349] PDF pages [10]
This version of the database will be referred to as the deslanted database.

[P23-S00350] PDF pages [10]
In the third version of the database, used in some early experiments, the images were reduced to 16x16 pixels.

[P23-S00351] PDF pages [10]
Several versions of LeNet-5 were trained on the regular MNIST database.

[P23-S00352] PDF pages [10]
Size-normalized examples from the MNIST database.

[P23-S00353] PDF pages [10]
Before each iteration, the diagonal Hessian approximation was reevaluated on 500 samples, as described in Appendix C and kept fixed during the entire iteration.

[P23-S00354] PDF pages [10]
The parameter pz was set to 0.02.

[P23-S00355] PDF pages [10]
The resulting effective learning rates during the first pass varied between approximately 7 x 10-° and 0.016 over the set of parameters.

[P23-S00356] PDF pages [10]
The test error rate stabilizes after around 10 passes through the training set at 0.95%.

[P23-S00357] PDF pages [10]
The error rate on the training set reaches 0.35% after 19 passes.

[P23-S00358] PDF pages [10]
Many authors have reported observing the common phenomenon of overtraining when training neural networks or other adaptive algorithms on various tasks.

[P23-S00359] PDF pages [10]
When over-training occurs, the training error keeps decreasing over time, but the test error goes through a minimum and starts increasing after a certain number of iterations.

[P23-S00360] PDF pages [10]
While this phenomenon is very common, it was not observed in our case as the learning curves in figure 5 show.

[P23-S00361] PDF pages [10]
A possible reason is that the learning rate was kept relatively large.

[P23-S00362] PDF pages [10]
The effect of this is that the weights never settle down in the local minimum but keep oscillating randomly.

[P23-S00363] PDF pages [10]
Because of those fluctuations, the average cost will be lower in a broader minimum.

[P23-S00364] PDF pages [10]
Therefore, stochastic gradient will have a similar effect as a regularization term that favors broader minima.

[P23-S00365] PDF pages [10]
Broader minima correspond to solutions with large entropy of the parameter distribution, which is beneficial to the generalization error.

[P23-S00366] PDF pages [10]
The influence of the training set size was measured by training the network with 15,000, 30,000, and 60,000 examples.

[P23-S00367] PDF pages [10]
The resulting training error and test error are shown in figure 6.

[P23-S00368] PDF pages [10]
It is clear that, even with specialized architectures such as LeNet-5, more training data would improve the accuracy.

[P23-S00369] PDF pages [10]
To verify this hypothesis, we artificially generated more training examples by randomly distorting the original training images.

[P23-S00370] PDF pages [11]
Training and test error of LeNet-5 as a function of the number of passes through the 60,000 pattern training set (without distortions).

[P23-S00371] PDF pages [11]
The average training error is measured on-the-fly as training proceeds.

[P23-S00372] PDF pages [11]
This explains why the training error appears to be larger than the test error.

[P23-S00373] PDF pages [11]
Convergence is attained after 10 to 12 passes through the training set.

[P23-S00374] PDF pages [11]
Training and test errors of LeNet-5 achieved using training sets of various sizes.

[P23-S00375] PDF pages [11]
This graph suggests that a larger training set could improve the performance of LeNet-5.

[P23-S00376] PDF pages [11]
The hollow square show the test error when more training patterns are artificially generated using random distortions.

[P23-S00377] PDF pages [11]
The test patterns are not distorted.

[P23-S00378] PDF pages [11]
The distortions were combinations of the following planar affine transformations: horizontal and vertical translations, scaling, squeezing (simultaneous horizontal compression and vertical elongation, or the reverse), and horizontal shearing.

[P23-S00379] PDF pages [11]
When distorted data was used for training, the test error rate dropped to 0.8% (from 0.95% without deformation).

[P23-S00380] PDF pages [11]
The same training parameters were used as without deformations.

[P23-S00381] PDF pages [11]
The total length of the training session was left unchanged (20 passes of 60,000 patterns each).

[P23-S00382] PDF pages [11]
It is interesting to note that the network effectively sees each individual sample only twice over the course of these 20 passes.

[P23-S00383] PDF pages [11]
Examples of distortions of ten training patterns.

[P23-S00384] PDF pages [11]
The 82 test patterns misclassified by LeNet-5.

[P23-S00385] PDF pages [11]
Below each image is displayed the correct answers (left) and the network answer (right).

[P23-S00386] PDF pages [11]
These errors are mostly caused either by genuinely ambiguous patterns, or by digits written in a style that are underrepresented in the training set.

[P23-S00387] PDF pages [11]
This shows that further improvements are to be expected with more training data.

[P23-S00388] PDF pages [11]
For the sake of comparison, a variety of other trainable classifiers was trained and tested on the same database.

[P23-S00389] PDF pages [11]
An early subset of these results was presented in [51].

[P23-S00390] PDF pages [11]
The error rates on the test set for the various methods are shown in figure 9.

[P23-S00391] PDF pages [11]
C.1 Linear Classifier, and Pairwise Linear Classifier Possibly the simplest classifier that one might consider is a linear classifier.

[P23-S00392] PDF pages [11]
Each input pixel value contributes to a weighted sum for each output unit.

[P23-S00393] PDF pages [12]
Error rate on the test set (%) for various classification methods.

[P23-S00394] PDF pages [12]
The uncertainty in the quoted error rates is about 0.1%.

[P23-S00395] PDF pages [12]
On the regular data, the error rate is 12%.

[P23-S00396] PDF pages [12]
On the deslanted images, the test error rate is 8.4% The network has 4010 free parameters.

[P23-S00397] PDF pages [12]
The deficiencies of the linear classifier are well documented [1] and it is included here simply to form a basis of comparison for more sophisticated classifiers.

[P23-S00398] PDF pages [12]
Various combinations of sigmoid units, linear units, gradient descent learning, and learning by directly solving linear systems gave similar re-sults.

[P23-S00399] PDF pages [12]
A simple improvement of the basic linear classifier was tested [52].

[P23-S00400] PDF pages [12]
The idea is to train each unit of a single-layer network to separate each class from each other class.

[P23-S00401] PDF pages [12]
Unit i/j is trained to produce +1 on patterns of class 7, -1 on patterns of class j, and is not trained on other patterns.

[P23-S00402] PDF pages [12]
The final score for class 7 is the sum of the outputs all the units labeled i/x minus the sum of the output of all the units labeled y/i, for all x and y.

[P23-S00403] PDF pages [12]
The error rate on the regular test set was 7.6%.

[P23-S00404] PDF pages [12]
Another simple classifier is a K-nearest neighbor classifier with a Euclidean distance measure between input images.

[P23-S00405] PDF pages [12]
This classifier has the advantage that no training time, and no brain on the part of the designer, are required.

[P23-S00406] PDF pages [12]
However, the memory requirement and recognition time are large: the complete 60,000 twenty by twenty pixel training images (about 24 Megabytes at one byte per pixel) must be available at run time.

[P23-S00407] PDF pages [12]
Much more compact representations could be devised with modest increase in error rate.

[P23-S00408] PDF pages [12]
On the regular test set the error rate was 5.0%.

[P23-S00409] PDF pages [12]
On the deslanted data, the error rate was 2.4%, with k = 3.

[P23-S00410] PDF pages [12]
Naturally, a realistic Euclidean distance nearest-neighbor system would operate on feature vectors rather than directly on the pixels, but since all of the other systems presented in this study operate directly on the pixels, this result is useful for a baseline comparison.

[P23-S00411] PDF pages [12]
C.3 Principal Component Analysis (PCA) and Polynomial Classifier Following [53], [54], a preprocessing stage was constructed which computes the projection of the input pattern on the 40 principal components of the set of training vectors.

[P23-S00412] PDF pages [12]
To compute the principal components, the mean of each input component was first computed and subtracted from the training vectors.

[P23-S00413] PDF pages [12]
The covariance matrix of the re-sulting vectors was then computed and diagonalized using Singular Value Decomposition.

[P23-S00414] PDF pages [12]
The 40-dimensional feature vector was used as the input of a second degree polynomial classifier.

[P23-S00415] PDF pages [12, 13]
This classifier can be seen as a linear classifier with 821 inputs, preceded by a module that computes all products of pairs of input variables.

[P23-S00416] PDF pages [13]
The error on the regular test set was 3.3%.

[P23-S00417] PDF pages [13]
Following [55], an RBF network was constructed.

[P23-S00418] PDF pages [13]
The first layer was composed of 1,000 Gaussian RBF units with 28x28 inputs, and the second layer was a simple 1000 inputs / 10 outputs linear classifier.

[P23-S00419] PDF pages [13]
The RBF units were divided into 10 groups of 100.

[P23-S00420] PDF pages [13]
Each group of units was trained on all the training examples of one of the 10 classes using the adaptive K-means algorithm.

[P23-S00421] PDF pages [13]
The second layer weights were computed using a regularized pseudo-inverse method.

[P23-S00422] PDF pages [13]
The error rate on the regular test set was 3.6% C.5 One-Hidden Layer Fully Connected Multilayer Neural Network Another classifier that we tested was a fully connected multi-layer neural network with two layers of weights (one hidden layer) trained with the version of back-propagation described in Appendix C. Error on the regular test set was 4.7% for a network with 300 hidden units, and 4.5% for a network with 1000 hidden units.

[P23-S00423] PDF pages [13]
Using artificial distortions to generate more training data brought only marginal improvement: 3.6% for 300 hidden units, and 3.8% for 1000 hidden units.

[P23-S00424] PDF pages [13]
When deslanted images were used, the test error jumped down to 1.6% for a network with 300 hidden units.

[P23-S00425] PDF pages [13]
It remains somewhat of a mystery that networks with such a large number of free parameters manage to achieve reasonably low testing errors.

[P23-S00426] PDF pages [13]
We conjecture that the dynamics of gradient descent learning in multilayer nets has a “self-regularization” effect.

[P23-S00427] PDF pages [13]
Because the origin of weight space is a saddle point that is attractive in almost every direction, the weights invariably shrink during the first few epochs (recent theoretical analysis seem to confirm this [56]).

[P23-S00428] PDF pages [13]
Small weights cause the sigmoids to operate in the quasi-linear region, making the network essentially equivalent to a low-capacity, single-layer network.

[P23-S00429] PDF pages [13]
As the learning proceeds, the weights grow, which progressively increases the effective capacity of the network.

[P23-S00430] PDF pages [13]
This seems to be an almost perfect, if fortuitous, implementation of Vapnik's “Structural Risk Minimization” principle [6].

[P23-S00431] PDF pages [13]
A better theoretical understanding of these phenomena, and more empirical evidence, are definitely needed.

[P23-S00432] PDF pages [13]
C.6 Two-Hidden Layer Fully Connected Multilayer Neural Network To see the effect of the architecture, several two-hidden layer multilayer neural networks were trained.

[P23-S00433] PDF pages [13]
Theoretical results have shown that any function can be approximated by a one-hidden layer neural network [57].

[P23-S00434] PDF pages [13]
However, several authors have observed that two-hidden layer architectures sometimes yield better performance in practical situations.

[P23-S00435] PDF pages [13]
This phenomenon was also observed here.

[P23-S00436] PDF pages [13]
The test error rate of a 28x28-300-100-10 network was 3.05%, a much better result than the one-hidden layer network, obtained using marginally more weights and connections.

[P23-S00437] PDF pages [13]
Increasing the network size to 28x28-1000-150-10 yielded only marginally improved error rates: 2.95%.

[P23-S00438] PDF pages [13]
C.7 A Small Convolutional Network: LeNet-1 Convolutional Networks are an attempt to solve the dilemma between small networks that cannot learn the training set, and large networks that seem overparameterized.

[P23-S00439] PDF pages [13]
LeNet-1 was an early embodiment of the Convolutional Network architecture which is included here for comparison purposes.

[P23-S00440] PDF pages [13]
The images were down-sampled to 16x16 pixels and centered in the 28x28 input layer.

[P23-S00441] PDF pages [13]
Although about 100,000 multiply/add steps are required to evaluate LeNet-1, its convolutional nature keeps the number of free parameters to only about 2600.

[P23-S00442] PDF pages [13]
The LeNet- 1 architecture was developed using our own version of the USPS (US Postal Service zip codes) database and its size was tuned to match the available data [35].

[P23-S00443] PDF pages [13]
The fact that a network with such a small number of parameters can attain such a good error rate is an indication that the architecture is appropriate for the task.

[P23-S00444] PDF pages [13]
Experiments with LeNet-1 made it clear that a larger convolutional network was needed to make optimal use of the large size of the training set.

[P23-S00445] PDF pages [13]
LeNet-4 and later LeNet- 5 were designed to address this problem.

[P23-S00446] PDF pages [13]
LeNet-4 is very similar to LeNet-5, except for the details of the architecture.

[P23-S00447] PDF pages [13]
It contains 4 first-level feature maps, followed by 8 subsampling maps connected in pairs to each first-layer feature maps, then 16 feature maps, followed by 16 subsampling map, followed by a fully connected layer with 120 units, followed by the output layer (10 units).

[P23-S00448] PDF pages [13]
LeNet-4 contains about 260,000 connections and has about 17,000 free parameters.

[P23-S00449] PDF pages [13]
In a series of experiments, we replaced the last layer of LeNet-4 with a Euclidean Nearest Neighbor classifier, and with the “local learning” method of Bottou and Vapnik [58], in which a local linear classifier is retrained each time a new test pattern is shown.

[P23-S00450] PDF pages [13]
Neither of those methods improved the raw error rate, although they did improve the rejection performance.

[P23-S00451] PDF pages [13]
Following theoretical work by R. Schapire [59], Drucker et al. [60] developed the “boosting” method for combining multiple classifiers.

[P23-S00452] PDF pages [13]
Three LeNet-4s are combined: the first one is trained the usual way.

[P23-S00453] PDF pages [13]
Finally, the third net is trained on new patterns on which the first and the second nets disagree.

[P23-S00454] PDF pages [13]
During testing, the outputs of the three nets are simply added.

[P23-S00455] PDF pages [13]
Because the error rate of LeNet-4 is very low, it was necessary to use the artificially distorted images (as with LeNet-5) in order to get enough samples to train the second and third nets.

[P23-S00456] PDF pages [13, 14]
The test error rate was 0.7%, the best of any of our classifiers.

[P23-S00457] PDF pages [14]
At first glance, boosting appears to be three times more expensive as a single net.

[P23-S00458] PDF pages [14]
In fact, when the first net produces a high confidence answer, the other nets are not called.

[P23-S00459] PDF pages [14]
The average computational cost is about 1.75 times that of a single net.

[P23-S00460] PDF pages [14]
The Tangent Distance classifier (TDC) is a nearestneighbor method where the distance function is made in-sensitive to small distortions and translations of the input image [61].

[P23-S00461] PDF pages [14]
If we consider an image as a point in a high dimensional pixel space (where the dimensionality equals the number of pixels), then an evolving distortion of a character traces out a curve in pixel space.

[P23-S00462] PDF pages [14]
Taken together, all these distortions define a low-dimensional manifold in pixel space.

[P23-S00463] PDF pages [14]
For small distortions, in the vicinity of the original image, this manifold can be approximated by a plane, known as the tangent plane.

[P23-S00464] PDF pages [14]
An excellent measure of 'closeness” for character images is the distance between their tangent planes, where the set of distortions used to generate the planes includes translations, scaling, skewing, squeezing, rotation, and line thickness variations.

[P23-S00465] PDF pages [14]
A test error rate of 1.1% was achieved using 16x16 pixel images.

[P23-S00466] PDF pages [14]
Prefiltering techniques using simple Euclidean distance at multiple resolutions allowed to reduce the number of necessary Tangent Distance calculations.

[P23-S00467] PDF pages [14]
Polynomial classifiers are well-studied methods for generating complex decision surfaces.

[P23-S00468] PDF pages [14]
Unfortunately, they are impractical for high-dimensional problems, because the number of product terms is prohibitive.

[P23-S00469] PDF pages [14]
The Support Vector technique is an extremely economical way of representing complex surfaces in high-dimensional spaces, including polynomials and many other types of surfaces [6].

[P23-S00470] PDF pages [14]
A particularly interesting subset of decision surfaces is the ones that correspond to hyperplanes that are at a maximum distance from the convex hulls of the two classes in the high-dimensional space of the product terms.

[P23-S00471] PDF pages [14]
Boser, Guyon, and Vapnik [62] realized that any polynomial of degree k in this “maximum margin” set can be computed by first computing the dot product of the input image with a subset of the training samples (called the “support vectors”), elevating the result to the k-th power, and linearly combining the numbers thereby obtained.

[P23-S00472] PDF pages [14]
Finding the support vectors and the coefficients amounts to solving a high-dimensional quadratic minimization problem with linear inequality constraints.

[P23-S00473] PDF pages [14]
For the sake of comparison, we in-clude here the results obtained by Burges and Schélkopf reported in [63].

[P23-S00474] PDF pages [14]
With a regular SVM, their error rate on the regular test set was 1.4%.

[P23-S00475] PDF pages [14]
Cortes and Vapnik had reported an error rate of 1.1% with SVM on the same data using a slightly different technique.

[P23-S00476] PDF pages [14]
The computational cost of this technique is very high: about 14 million multiply-adds per recognition.

[P23-S00477] PDF pages [14]
Using Schédlkopf's Virtual Support Vectors technique (V-SVM), 1.0% error was attained.

[P23-S00478] PDF pages [14]
Rejection Performance: percentage of test patterns that must be rejected to achieve 0.5% error for some of the systems.

[P23-S00479] PDF pages [14]
Number of multiply-accumulate operations for the recognition of a single character starting with a size-normalized image.

[P23-S00480] PDF pages [14]
Unfortunately, V-SVM is extremely expensive: about twice as much as regular SVM.

[P23-S00481] PDF pages [14]
To alleviate this problem, Burges has proposed the Reduced Set Support Vector technique (RS-SVM), which attained 1.1% on the regular test set [63], with a computational cost of only 650,000 multiply-adds per recognition, i.e. only about 60% more expensive than LeNet-5.

[P23-S00482] PDF pages [14]
A summary of the performance of the classifiers is shown in Figures 9 to 12.

[P23-S00483] PDF pages [14]
Boosted LeNet-4 performed best, achieving a score of 0.7%, closely followed by LeNet-5 at 0.8%.

[P23-S00484] PDF pages [14]
Patterns are rejected when the value of corresponding output is smaller than a predefined threshold.

[P23-S00485] PDF pages [14]
In many applications, rejection performance is more significant than raw error rate.

[P23-S00486] PDF pages [14]
The score used to decide upon the rejection of a pattern was the difference between the scores of the top two classes.

[P23-S00487] PDF pages [14]
Again, Boosted LeNet-4 has the best performance.

[P23-S00488] PDF pages [15]
Memory requirements, measured in number of variables, for each of the methods.

[P23-S00489] PDF pages [15]
Most of the methods only require one byte per variable for adequate performance.

[P23-S00490] PDF pages [15]
Expectedly, neural networks are much less demanding than memory-based methods.

[P23-S00491] PDF pages [15]
Convolutional Neural Networks are particularly well suited to hardware implementations because of their regular structure and their low memory requirements for the weights.

[P23-S00492] PDF pages [15]
Single chip mixed analog-digital implementations of LeNet-5's predecessors have been shown to operate at speeds in excess of 1000 characters per second [64].

[P23-S00493] PDF pages [15]
However, the rapid progress of mainstream computer technology renders those exotic technologies quickly obsolete.

[P23-S00494] PDF pages [15]
Cost-effective implementations of memory-based techniques are more elusive, due to their enormous memory requirements, and computational requirements.

[P23-S00495] PDF pages [15]
K-nearest neighbors and TDC have essentially zero training time.

[P23-S00496] PDF pages [15]
While the single-layer net, the pairwise net, and PCA+quadratic net could be trained in less than an hour, the multilayer net training times were expectedly much longer, but only re-quired 10 to 20 passes through the training set.

[P23-S00497] PDF pages [15]
This amounts to 2 to 3 days of CPU to train LeNet-5 on a Silicon Graphics Origin 2000 server, using a single 200MHz R10000 processor.

[P23-S00498] PDF pages [15]
It is important to note that while the training time is somewhat relevant to the designer, it is of little interest to the final user of the system.

[P23-S00499] PDF pages [15]
Given the choice between an existing technique, and a new technique that brings marginal accuracy improvements at the price of considerable training time, any final user would chose the latter.

[P23-S00500] PDF pages [15]
Most methods require only about one byte per variable for adequate performance.

[P23-S00501] PDF pages [15]
However, Nearest- Neighbor methods may get by with 4 bits per pixel for stor- ing the template images.

[P23-S00502] PDF pages [15]
Not surprisingly, neural networks require much less memory than memory-based methods.

[P23-S00503] PDF pages [15]
The Overall performance depends on many factors in-cluding accuracy, running time, and memory requirements.

[P23-S00504] PDF pages [15]
As computer technology improves, larger-capacity recognizers become feasible.

[P23-S00505] PDF pages [15]
Larger recognizers in turn require larger training sets.

[P23-S00506] PDF pages [15]
LeNet-1 was appropriate to the available technology in 1989, just as LeNet-5 is appropriate now.

[P23-S00507] PDF pages [15]
In 1989 a recognizer as complex as LeNet-5 would have re-quired several weeks' training, and more data than was available, and was therefore not even considered.

[P23-S00508] PDF pages [15]
For quite a long time, LeNet-1 was considered the state of the art.

[P23-S00509] PDF pages [15]
The local learning classifier, the optimal margin classifier, and the tangent distance classifier were developed to improve upon LeNet-1 - and they succeeded at that.

[P23-S00510] PDF pages [15]
However, they in turn motivated a search for improved neural network architectures.

[P23-S00511] PDF pages [15]
This search was guided in part by estimates of the capacity of various learning machines, derived from measurements of the training and test error as a function of the number of training examples.

[P23-S00512] PDF pages [15]
We discovered that more capacity was needed.

[P23-S00513] PDF pages [15]
Through a series of experiments in architecture, combined with an analysis of the characteristics of recognition errors, LeNet-4 and LeNet-5 were crafted.

[P23-S00514] PDF pages [15]
We find that boosting gives a substantial improvement in accuracy, with a relatively modest penalty in memory and computing expense.

[P23-S00515] PDF pages [15]
Also, distortion models can be used to increase the effective size of a data set without actually requiring to collect more data.

[P23-S00516] PDF pages [15]
The Support Vector Machine has excellent accuracy, which is most remarkable, because unlike the other high performance classifiers, it does not include a priori knowledge about the problem.

[P23-S00517] PDF pages [15]
In fact, this classifier would do just as well if the image pixels were permuted with a fixed mapping and lost their pictorial structure.

[P23-S00518] PDF pages [15]
However, reaching levels of performance comparable to the Convolutional Neural Networks can only be done at considerable expense in memory and computational requirements.

[P23-S00519] PDF pages [15]
The reducedset SVM requirements are within a factor of two of the Convolutional Networks, and the error rate is very close.

[P23-S00520] PDF pages [15]
Improvements of those results are expected, as the technique is relatively new.

[P23-S00521] PDF pages [15]
When plenty of data is available, many methods can attain respectable accuracy.

[P23-S00522] PDF pages [15]
The neural-net methods run much faster and require much less space than memorybased techniques.

[P23-S00523] PDF pages [15]
The neural nets' advantage will become more striking as training databases continue to increase in size.

[P23-S00524] PDF pages [15]
Convolutional networks are particularly well suited for recognizing or rejecting shapes with widely varying size, position, and orientation, such as the ones typically produced by heuristic segmenters in real-world string recognition systems.

[P23-S00525] PDF pages [15]
In an experiment like the one described above, the importance of noise resistance and distortion invariance is not obvious.

[P23-S00526] PDF pages [15, 16]
The situation in most real applications is quite different.

[P23-S00527] PDF pages [16]
Characters must generally be segmented out of their context prior to recognition.

[P23-S00528] PDF pages [16]
Segmentation algorithms are rarely perfect and often leave extraneous marks in character images (noise, underlines, neighboring characters), or sometimes cut characters too much and produce incomplete characters.

[P23-S00529] PDF pages [16]
Those images cannot be re-liably size-normalized and centered.

[P23-S00530] PDF pages [16]
Normalizing incomplete characters can be very dangerous.

[P23-S00531] PDF pages [16]
For example, an enlarged stray mark can look like a genuine 1.

[P23-S00532] PDF pages [16]
Therefore many systems have resorted to normalizing the images at the level of fields or words.

[P23-S00533] PDF pages [16]
In our case, the upper and lower profiles of entire fields (amounts in a check) are detected and used to normalize the image to a fixed height.

[P23-S00534] PDF pages [16]
While this guarantees that stray marks will not be blown up into character-looking images, this also creates wide variations of the size and vertical position of characters after segmentation.

[P23-S00535] PDF pages [16]
Therefore it is preferable to use a recognizer that is robust to such variations.

[P23-S00536] PDF pages [16]
It is estimated that accurate recognition occurs for scale variations up to about a factor of 2, vertical shift variations of plus or minus about half the height of the character, and rotations up to plus or minus 30 degrees.

[P23-S00537] PDF pages [16]
While fully invariant recognition of complex shapes is still an elusive goal, it seems that Convolutional Networks offer a partial answer to the problem of invariance or robustness with respect to geometrical distortions.

[P23-S00538] PDF pages [16]
Processing those images would pose unsurmountable problems of segmentation and feature extraction to many methods, but LeNet-5 seems able to robustly extract salient features from these cluttered images.

[P23-S00539] PDF pages [16]
The training set used for the network shown here was the MNIST training set with salt and pepper noise added.

[P23-S00540] PDF pages [16]
Each pixel was randomly inverted with probability 0.1.

[P23-S00541] PDF pages [16]
MuLTI-MoDULE SYSTEMS AND GRAPH TRANSFORMER NETWORKS The classical back-propagation algorithm, as described and used in the previous sections, is a simple form of Gradient-Based Learning.

[P23-S00542] PDF pages [16]
However, it is clear that the gradient back-propagation algorithm given by Equation 4 describes a more general situation than simple multi-layer feed-forward networks composed of alternated linear transformations and sigmoidal functions.

[P23-S00543] PDF pages [16]
In principle, derivatives can be back-propagated through any arrangement of functional modules, as long as we can compute the product of the Jacobians of those modules by any vector.

[P23-S00544] PDF pages [16]
Why would we want to train systems composed of multiple heterogeneous modules?

[P23-S00545] PDF pages [16]
The answer is that large and complex trainable systems need to be built out of simple, specialized modules.

[P23-S00546] PDF pages [16]
The simplest example is LeNet-5, which mixes convolutional layers, sub-sampling layers, fully-connected layers, and RBF layers.

[P23-S00547] PDF pages [16]
A trainable system composed of heterogeneous modules.

[P23-S00548] PDF pages [16]
A multi-module system is defined by the function implemented by each of the modules, and by the graph of interconnection of the modules to each other.

[P23-S00549] PDF pages [16]
The graph implicitly defines a partial order according to which the modules must be updated in the forward pass.

[P23-S00550] PDF pages [16]
For example in Figure 14, module 0 is first updated, then modules 1 and 2 are updated (possibly in parallel), and finally module 3.

[P23-S00551] PDF pages [16]
Modules may or may not have trainable parameters.

[P23-S00552] PDF pages [16]
Loss functions, which measure the performance of the system, are implemented as module 4.

[P23-S00553] PDF pages [16]
In the simplest case, the loss function module receives an external input that carries the desired output.

[P23-S00554] PDF pages [16]
