In this framework, there is no qualitative difference between trainable parameters (W1,W2 in the figure), external inputs and outputs (Z,D,E), and intermediate state variables(X1,X2,X3,X4,X5).

[P23-S00555] PDF pages [16]
An Object-Oriented Approach Object-Oriented programming offers a particularly convenient way of implementing multi-module systems.

[P23-S00556] PDF pages [16]
Each module is an instance of a class.

[P23-S00557] PDF pages [16]
Module classes have a “forward propagation” method (or member function) called fprop whose arguments are the inputs and outputs of the module.

[P23-S00558] PDF pages [16]
For example, computing the output of module 3 in Figure 14 can be done by calling the method fprop on module 3 with the arguments X3,X4,X5.

[P23-S00559] PDF pages [16]
Complex modules can be constructed from simpler modules by simply defining a new class whose slots will contain the member modules and the intermediate state variables between those modules.

[P23-S00560] PDF pages [16]
The fprop method for the class simply calls the fprop methods of the member modules, with the appropriate intermediate state variables or external input and outputs as arguments.

[P23-S00561] PDF pages [16]
Although the algorithms are easily generalizable to any network of such modules, including those whose influence graph has cycles, we will limit the discussion to the case of directed acyclic graphs (feed-forward networks).

[P23-S00562] PDF pages [16]
Computing derivatives in a multi-module system is just as simple.

[P23-S00563] PDF pages [16]
A “backward propagation” method, called bprop, for each module class can be defined for that purpose.

[P23-S00564] PDF pages [17]
Examples of unusual, distorted, and noisy characters correctly recognized by LeNet-5.

[P23-S00565] PDF pages [17]
The grey-level of the output label represents the penalty (lighter for higher penalties).

[P23-S00566] PDF pages [17]
All the derivatives in the system can be computed by calling the bprop method on all the modules in reverse order compared to the forward propagation phase.

[P23-S00567] PDF pages [17]
The state variables are assumed to contain slots for storing the gradients computed during the backward pass, in addition to storage for the states computed in the forward pass.

[P23-S00568] PDF pages [17]
The backward pass effectively computes the partial derivatives of the loss E with respect to all the state variables and all the parameters in the system.

[P23-S00569] PDF pages [17]
There is an interesting duality property between the forward and backward functions of certain modules.

[P23-S00570] PDF pages [17]
For example, a sum of several variables in the forward direction is transformed into a simple fan-out (replication) in the backward direction.

[P23-S00571] PDF pages [17]
Conversely, a fan-out in the forward direction is transformed into a sum in the backward direction.

[P23-S00572] PDF pages [17]
The software environment used to obtain the results described in this paper, called SN3.1, uses the above concepts.

[P23-S00573] PDF pages [17]
It is based on a home-grown object-oriented dialect of Lisp with a compiler to C. The fact that derivatives can be computed by propagation in the reverse graph is easy to understand intuitively.

[P23-S00574] PDF pages [17]
The best way to justify it theoretically is through the use of Lagrange functions [21], [22].

[P23-S00575] PDF pages [17]
The same formalism can be used to extend the procedures to networks with recurrent connections.

[P23-S00576] PDF pages [17]
Neural networks and many other standard pattern recognition techniques can be formulated in terms of multi-modular systems trained with Gradient-Based Learning.

[P23-S00577] PDF pages [17]
Commonly used modules include matrix multiplications and sigmoidal modules, the combination of which can be used to build conventional neural networks.

[P23-S00578] PDF pages [17]
Other modules include convolutional layers, sub-sampling layers, RBF layers, and “softmax” layers [65].

[P23-S00579] PDF pages [17]
Loss functions are also represented as modules whose single output produces the value of the loss.

[P23-S00580] PDF pages [17]
Commonly used modules have simple bprop methods.

[P23-S00581] PDF pages [17]
In general, the bprop method of a function F is a multiplication by the Jacobian of F'.

[P23-S00582] PDF pages [17]
Here are a few commonly used examples.

[P23-S00583] PDF pages [17]
The bprop method of a fanout (a “Y” connection) is a sum, and vice versa.

[P23-S00584] PDF pages [17]
The bprop method of a multiplication by a coefficient is a multiplication by the same coefficient.

[P23-S00585] PDF pages [17]
The bprop method of a multiplication by a matrix is a multiplication by the transpose of that matrix.

[P23-S00586] PDF pages [17]
The bprop method of an addition with a constant is the identity.

[P23-S00587] PDF pages [18]
Traditional neural networks, and multi-module systems communicate fixed-size vectors between layer.

[P23-S00588] PDF pages [18]
Multi-Layer Graph Transformer Networks are composed of trainable modules that operate on and produce graphs whose arcs carry numerical in-formation.

[P23-S00589] PDF pages [18]
Interestingly, certain non-differentiable modules can be inserted in a multi-module system without adverse effect.

[P23-S00590] PDF pages [18]
An interesting example of that is the multiplexer module.

[P23-S00591] PDF pages [18]
It has two (or more) regular inputs, one switching input, and one output.

[P23-S00592] PDF pages [18]
The module selects one of its inputs, depending upon the (discrete) value of the switching input, and copies it on its output.

[P23-S00593] PDF pages [18]
While this module is not differentiable with respect to the switching input, it is differentiable with respect to the regular inputs.

[P23-S00594] PDF pages [18]
Therefore the overall function of a system that includes such modules will be differentiable with respect to its parameters as long as the switching input does not depend upon the parameters.

[P23-S00595] PDF pages [18]
For example, the switching input can be an external input.

[P23-S00596] PDF pages [18]
Another interesting case is the min module.

[P23-S00597] PDF pages [18]
This module has two (or more) inputs and one output.

[P23-S00598] PDF pages [18]
The output of the module is the minimum of the inputs.

[P23-S00599] PDF pages [18]
The function of this module is differentiable everywhere, except on the switching surface which is a set of measure zero.

[P23-S00600] PDF pages [18]
In-terestingly, this function is continuous and reasonably regular, and that is sufficient to ensure the convergence of a Gradient-Based Learning algorithm.

[P23-S00601] PDF pages [18]
The object-oriented implementation of the multi-module idea can easily be extended to include a bbprop method that propagates Gauss-Newton approximations of the second derivatives.

[P23-S00602] PDF pages [18]
This leads to a direct generalization for modular systems of the second-derivative back-propagation Equation 22 given in the Appendix.

[P23-S00603] PDF pages [18]
The multiplexer module is a special case of a much more general situation, described at length in Section VIII, where the architecture of the system changes dynamically with the input data.

[P23-S00604] PDF pages [18]
Multiplexer modules can be used to dynamically rewire (or reconfigure) the architecture of the system for each new input pattern.

[P23-S00605] PDF pages [18]
Multi-module systems are a very flexible tool for building large trainable system.

[P23-S00606] PDF pages [18]
However, the descriptions in the previous sections implicitly assumed that the set of parameters, and the state information communicated be- tween the modules, are all fixed-size vectors.

[P23-S00607] PDF pages [18]
The limited flexibility of fixed-size vectors for data representation is a serious deficiency for many applications, notably for tasks that deal with variable length inputs (e.g continuous speech recognition and handwritten word recognition), or for tasks that require encoding relationships between objects or features whose number and nature can vary (invariant perception, scene analysis, recognition of composite objects).

[P23-S00608] PDF pages [18]
An important special case is the recognition of strings of characters or words.

[P23-S00609] PDF pages [18]
More generally, fixed-size vectors lack flexibility for tasks in which the state must encode probability distributions over sequences of vectors or symbols as is the case in linguistic processing.

[P23-S00610] PDF pages [18]
Such distributions over sequences are best represented by stochastic grammars, or, in the more general case, directed graphs in which each arc contains a vector (stochastic grammars are special cases in which the vector contains probabilities and symbolic information).

[P23-S00611] PDF pages [18]
Each path in the graph represents a different sequence of vectors.

[P23-S00612] PDF pages [18]
Distributions over sequences can be represented by interpreting elements of the data associated with each arc as parameters of a probability distribution or simply as a penalty.

[P23-S00613] PDF pages [18]
Distributions over sequences are particularly handy for modeling linguistic knowledge in speech or handwriting recognition systems: each sequence, i.e., each path in the graph, represents an alternative interpretation of the input.

[P23-S00614] PDF pages [18]
Successive processing modules progressively refine the interpretation.

[P23-S00615] PDF pages [18]
For example, a speech recognition system might start with a single sequence of acoustic vectors, transform it into a lattice of phonemes (distribution over phoneme sequences), then into a lattice of words (distribution over word sequences), and then into a single sequence of words representing the best interpretation.

[P23-S00616] PDF pages [18]
In our work on building large-scale handwriting recognition systems, we have found that these systems could much more easily and quickly be developed and designed by viewing the system as a networks of modules that take one or several graphs as input and produce graphs as out-put.

[P23-S00617] PDF pages [18]
Such modules are called Graph Transformers, and the complete systems are called Graph Transformer Networks, or GTN.

[P23-S00618] PDF pages [18]
Modules in a GTN communicate their states and gradients in the form of directed graphs whose arcs carry numerical information (scalars or vectors) [66].

[P23-S00619] PDF pages [18]
From the statistical point of view, the fixed-size state vectors of conventional networks can be seen as representing the means of distributions in state space.

[P23-S00620] PDF pages [18]
In variablesize networks such as the Space-Displacement Neural Networks described in section VII, the states are variablelength sequences of fixed size vectors.

[P23-S00621] PDF pages [18]
They can be seen as representing the mean of a probability distribution over variable-length sequences of fixed-size vectors.

[P23-S00622] PDF pages [18]
In GTNs, the states are represented as graphs, which can be seen as representing mixtures of probability distributions over structured collections (possibly sequences) of vectors (Figure 15).

[P23-S00623] PDF pages [19]
Gradient back-propagation through a Graph Transformer takes gradients with respect to the numerical information in the output graph, and computes gradients with re-spect to the numerical information attached to the input graphs, and with respect to the module's internal parameters.

[P23-S00624] PDF pages [19]
Gradient-Based Learning can be applied as long as differentiable functions are used to produce the numerical data in the output graph from the numerical data in the input graph and the functions parameters.

[P23-S00625] PDF pages [19]
The second point of the next several sections is to show that the functions implemented by many of the modules used in typical document processing systems (and other image recognition systems), though commonly thought to be combinatorial in nature, are indeed differentiable with respect to their internal parameters as well as with respect to their inputs, and are therefore usable as part of a globally trainable system.

[P23-S00626] PDF pages [19]
In most of the following, we will purposely avoid making references to probability theory.

[P23-S00627] PDF pages [19]
All the quantities manipulated are viewed as penalties, or costs, which if necessary can be transformed into probabilities by taking exponentials and normalizing.

[P23-S00628] PDF pages [19]
V. MULTIPLE OBJECT RECOGNITION: HEURISTIC OVER-SEGMENTATION One of the most difficult problems of handwriting recognition is to recognize not just isolated characters, but strings of characters, such as zip codes, check amounts, or words.

[P23-S00629] PDF pages [19]
Since most recognizers can only deal with one character at a time, we must first segment the string into individual character images.

[P23-S00630] PDF pages [19]
However, it is almost impossible to devise image analysis techniques that will infallibly segment naturally written sequences of characters into well formed characters.

[P23-S00631] PDF pages [19]
The recent history of automatic speech recognition [28], [67] is here to remind us that training a recognizer by optimizing a global criterion (at the word or sentence level) is much preferable to merely training it on hand-segmented phonemes or other units.

[P23-S00632] PDF pages [19]
Several recent works have shown that the same is true for handwriting recognition [38]: optimizing a word-level criterion is preferable to solely training a recognizer on pre-segmented characters because the recognizer can learn not only to recognize individual characters, but also to reject mis-segmented characters thereby minimizing the overall word error.

[P23-S00633] PDF pages [19]
This section and the next describe in detail a simple example of GTN to address the problem of reading strings of characters, such as words or check amounts.

[P23-S00634] PDF pages [19]
The method avoids the expensive and unreliable task of hand-truthing the result of the segmentation often required in more traditional systems trained on individually labeled character images.

[P23-S00635] PDF pages [19]
A now classical method for word segmentation and recognition is called Heuristic Over-Segmentation [68], [69].

[P23-S00636] PDF pages [19]
Building a segmentation graph with Heuristic Over- Segmentation.

[P23-S00637] PDF pages [19]
The idea is to use heuristic image processing techniques to find candidate cuts of the word or string, and then to use the recognizer to score the alternative segmentations thereby generated.

[P23-S00638] PDF pages [19]
The process is depicted in Figure 16.

[P23-S00639] PDF pages [19]
First, a number of candidate cuts are generated.

[P23-S00640] PDF pages [19]
Good candidate locations for cuts can be found by locating minima in the vertical projection profile, or minima of the distance between the upper and lower contours of the word.

[P23-S00641] PDF pages [19]
Better segmentation heuristics are described in section X.

[P23-S00642] PDF pages [19]
The cut generation heuristic is designed so as to generate more cuts than necessary, in the hope that the “correct” set of cuts will be included.

[P23-S00643] PDF pages [19]
Once the cuts have been generated, alternative segmentations are best represented by a graph, called the segmentation graph.

[P23-S00644] PDF pages [19]
The segmentation graph is a Directed Acyclic Graph (DAG) with a start node and an end node.

[P23-S00645] PDF pages [19]
Each internal node is associated with a candidate cut produced by the segmentation algorithm.

[P23-S00646] PDF pages [19]
Each arc between a source node and a destination node is associated with an image that contains all the ink between the cut associated with the source node and the cut associated with the destination node.

[P23-S00647] PDF pages [19]
An arc is created between two nodes if the segmentor decided that the ink between the corresponding cuts could form a candidate character.

[P23-S00648] PDF pages [19]
Typically, each individual piece of ink would be associated with an arc.

[P23-S00649] PDF pages [19]
Pairs of successive pieces of ink would also be included, unless they are separated by a wide gap, which is a clear indication that they belong to different characters.

[P23-S00650] PDF pages [19]
Each complete path through the graph contains each piece of ink once and only once.

[P23-S00651] PDF pages [19]
Each path corresponds to a different way of associating pieces of ink together so as to form characters.

[P23-S00652] PDF pages [19]
Recognition Transformer and Viterbi Transformer A simple GTN to recognize character strings is shown in Figure 17.

[P23-S00653] PDF pages [19]
It is composed of two graph transformers called the recognition transformer Tyee, and the Viterbi transformer Ty.

[P23-S00654] PDF pages [19]
The goal of the recognition transformer is to generate a graph, called the interpretation graph or recognition graph Ginz, that contains all the possible interpretations for all the possible segmentations of the input.

[P23-S00655] PDF pages [19]
Each path in G,,; represents one possible interpretation of one particular segmentation of the input.

[P23-S00656] PDF pages [19]
The role of the Viterbi transformer is to extract the best interpretation from the interpretation graph.

[P23-S00657] PDF pages [20]
Recognizing a character string with a GTN.

[P23-S00658] PDF pages [20]
For readability, only the arcs with low penalties are shown.

[P23-S00659] PDF pages [20]
The interpretation graph Ging has almost the same structure as the segmentation graph, except that each arc is replaced by a set of arcs from and to the same node.

[P23-S00660] PDF pages [20]
In this set of arcs, there is one arc for each possible class for the image associated with the corresponding arc in Geg.

[P23-S00661] PDF pages [20]
As shown in Figure 18, to each arc is attached a class label, and the penalty that the image belongs to this class as produced by the recognizer.

[P23-S00662] PDF pages [20]
If the segmentor has computed penalties for the candidate segments, these penalties are combined with the penalties computed by the character recognizer, to obtain the penalties on the arcs of the interpretation graph.

[P23-S00663] PDF pages [20]
Although combining penalties of different nature seems highly heuristic, the GTN training procedure will tune the penalties and take advantage of this combination anyway.

[P23-S00664] PDF pages [20]
Each path in the interpretation graph corresponds to a possible interpretation of the input word.

[P23-S00665] PDF pages [20]
The penalty of a particular interpretation for a particular segmentation is given by the sum of the arc penalties along the corresponding path in the interpretation graph.

[P23-S00666] PDF pages [20]
Computing the penalty of an in-terpretation independently of the segmentation requires to combine the penalties of all the paths with that interpretation.

[P23-S00667] PDF pages [20]
An appropriate rule for combining the penalties of parallel paths is given in section VI-C.

[P23-S00668] PDF pages [20]
The Viterbi transformer produces a graph Gy with a single path.

[P23-S00669] PDF pages [20]
This path is the path of least cumulated penalty in the Interpretation graph.

[P23-S00670] PDF pages [20]
The result of the recognition can be produced by reading off the labels of the arcs along the graph Gy extracted by the Viterbi transformer.

[P23-S00671] PDF pages [20]
The recognition transformer refines each arc of the segmentation arc into a set of arcs in the interpretation graph, one per character class, with attached penalties and labels.

[P23-S00672] PDF pages [20]
Let c; be the penalty associated to arc i, with source node s;, and destination node d; (note that there can be multiple arcs between two nodes).

[P23-S00673] PDF pages [20]
In the interpretation graph, arcs also have a label 1;.

[P23-S00674] PDF pages [20]
The Viterbi algorithm proceeds as follows.

[P23-S00675] PDF pages [20]
Each node n is associated with a cumulated Viterbi penalty v,.

[P23-S00676] PDF pages [20]
Those cumulated penalties are computed in any order that satisfies the partial order defined by the interpretation graph (which is directed and acyclic).

[P23-S00677] PDF pages [20]
The start node is initialized with the cumulated penalty starz = 0.

[P23-S00678] PDF pages [20]
Furthermore, the value of 7 for each node n which minimizes the right hand side is noted m,, the minimizing entering arc.

[P23-S00679] PDF pages [20]
When the end node is reached we obtain in veng the total penalty of the path with the smallest total penalty.

[P23-S00680] PDF pages [20]
We call this penalty the Viterbi penalty, and this sequence of arcs and nodes the Viterbi path.

[P23-S00681] PDF pages [20]
The label sequence can then be read off the arcs of the Viterbi path.

[P23-S00682] PDF pages [21]
The previous section describes the process of recognizing a string using Heuristic Over-Segmentation, assuming that the recognizer is trained so as to give low penalties for the correct class label of correctly segmented characters, high penalties for erroneous categories of correctly segmented characters, and high penalties for all categories for badly formed characters.

[P23-S00683] PDF pages [21]
This section explains how to train the system at the string level to do the above without requiring manual labeling of character segments.

[P23-S00684] PDF pages [21]
This training will be performed with a GIN whose architecture is slightly different from the recognition architecture described in the previous section.

[P23-S00685] PDF pages [21]
In many applications, there is enough a priori knowledge about what is expected from each of the modules in order to train them separately.

[P23-S00686] PDF pages [21]
For example, with Heuristic Over-Segmentation one could individually label single-character images and train a character recognizer on them, but it might be difficult to obtain an appropriate set of non-character images to train the model to reject wrongly segmented candidates.

[P23-S00687] PDF pages [21]
Although separate training is simple, it requires additional supervision information that is often lacking or incomplete (the correct segmentation and the labels of incorrect candidate segments).

[P23-S00688] PDF pages [21]
Furthermore it can be shown that separate training is sub-optimal [67].

[P23-S00689] PDF pages [21]
The following section describes three different gradientbased methods for training GTN-based handwriting recognizers at the string level: Viterbi training, discriminative Viterbi training, forward training, and discriminative forward training.

[P23-S00690] PDF pages [21]
The last one is a generalization to graphbased systems of the MAP criterion introduced in Section II-C. Discriminative forward training is somewhat similar to the so-called Maximum Mutual Information criterion used to train HMM in speech recognition.

[P23-S00691] PDF pages [21]
However, our rationale differs from the classical one.

[P23-S00692] PDF pages [21]
We make no recourse to a probabilistic interpretation, but show that, within the Gradient-Based Learning approach, discriminative training is a simple instance of the pervasive principle of error correcting learning.

[P23-S00693] PDF pages [21]
Training methods for graph-based sequence recognition systems such as HMMs have been extensively studied in the context of speech recognition [28].

[P23-S00694] PDF pages [21]
Those methods re-quire that the system be based on probabilistic generative models of the data, which provide normalized likelihoods over the space of possible input sequences.

[P23-S00695] PDF pages [21]
Popular HMM learning methods, such as the the Baum-Welsh algorithm, rely on this normalization.

[P23-S00696] PDF pages [21]
The normalization cannot be preserved when non-generative models such as neural networks are integrated into the system.

[P23-S00697] PDF pages [21]
Other techniques, such as discriminative training methods, must be used in this case.

[P23-S00698] PDF pages [21]
Other globally trainable sequence recognition systems avoid the difficulties of statistical modeling by not resorting to graph-based techniques.

[P23-S00699] PDF pages [21]
Viterbi Training GTN Architecture for a character string recognizer based on Heuristic Over-Segmentation.

[P23-S00700] PDF pages [21]
Unfortunately, despite early enthusiasm, the training of RNNs with gradient-based techniques has proved very difficult in practice [79].

[P23-S00701] PDF pages [21]
The GTN techniques presented below simplify and generalize the global training methods developed for speech recognition.

[P23-S00702] PDF pages [21]
During recognition, we select the path in the Interpretation Graph that has the lowest penalty with the Viterbi algorithm.

[P23-S00703] PDF pages [21]
Ideally, we would like this path of lowest penalty to be associated with the correct label sequence as often as possible.

[P23-S00704] PDF pages [21]
An obvious loss function to minimize is therefore the average over the training set of the penalty of the path associated with the correct label sequence that has the low-est penalty.

[P23-S00705] PDF pages [21]
The goal of training will be to find the set of recognizer parameters (the weights, if the recognizer is a neural network) that minimize the average penalty of this “correct” lowest penalty path.

[P23-S00706] PDF pages [21]
The gradient of this loss function can be computed by back-propagation through the GTN architecture shown in figure 19.

[P23-S00707] PDF pages [21]
This training architecture is almost identical to the recognition architecture described in the previous section, except that an extra graph transformer called a path selector is inserted between the Interpretation Graph and the Viterbi Transformer.

[P23-S00708] PDF pages [21]
This transformer takes the interpretation graph and the desired label sequence as input.

[P23-S00709] PDF pages [21]
It extracts from the interpretation graph those paths that contain the correct (desired) label sequence.

[P23-S00710] PDF pages [21]
Its output graph G; is called the constrained interpretation graph (also known as forced alignment in the HMM literature), and contains all the paths that correspond to the correct label sequence.

[P23-S00711] PDF pages [21]
The constrained interpretation graph is then sent to the Viterbi transformer which produces a graph Gyyi, with a single path.

[P23-S00712] PDF pages [21]
This path is the “correct” path with the lowest penalty.

[P23-S00713] PDF pages [21]
Finally, a path scorer transformer takes Geyi,, and simply computes its cumulated penalty Cyyit by adding up the penalties along the path.

[P23-S00714] PDF pages [22]
The only label information that is required by the above system is the sequence of desired character labels.

[P23-S00715] PDF pages [22]
No knowledge of the correct segmentation is required on the part of the supervisor, since it chooses among the segmentations in the interpretation graph the one that yields the lowest penalty.

[P23-S00716] PDF pages [22]
The process of back-propagating gradients through the Viterbi training GTN is now described.

[P23-S00717] PDF pages [22]
As explained in section IV, the gradients must be propagated backwards through all modules of the GTN, in order to compute gradients in preceding modules and thereafter tune their parameters.

[P23-S00718] PDF pages [22]
Back-propagating gradients through the path scorer is quite straightforward.

[P23-S00719] PDF pages [22]
The partial derivatives of the loss function with respect to the individual penalties on the constrained Viterbi path G.yit are equal to 1, since the loss function is simply the sum of those penalties.

[P23-S00720] PDF pages [22]
Backpropagating through the Viterbi Transformer is equally simple.

[P23-S00721] PDF pages [22]
The partial derivatives of Ey, with respect to the penalties on the arcs of the constrained graph G, are 1 for those arcs that appear in the constrained Viterbi path Gevit, and 0 for those that do not.

[P23-S00722] PDF pages [22]
Why is it legitimate to back-propagate through an essentially discrete function such as the Viterbi Transformer?

[P23-S00723] PDF pages [22]
The answer is that the Viterbi Transformer is nothing more than a collection of min functions and adders put together.

[P23-S00724] PDF pages [22]
It was shown in Section IV that gradients can be back-propagated through min functions without adverse effects.

[P23-S00725] PDF pages [22]
Back-propagation through the path selector transformer is similar to backpropagation through the Viterbi transformer.

[P23-S00726] PDF pages [22]
Arcs in Gint that appear in G, have the same gradient as the corresponding arc in Gg, i.e. 1 or 0, depending on whether the arc appear in Geyiz.

[P23-S00727] PDF pages [22]
During the forward propagation through the recognition transformer, one in-stance of the recognizer for single character was created for each arc in the segmentation graph.

[P23-S00728] PDF pages [22]
The state of recognizer instances was stored.

[P23-S00729] PDF pages [22]
Since each arc penalty in Gint is produced by an individual output of a recognizer instance, we now have a gradient (1 or 0) for each out-put of each instance of the recognizer.

[P23-S00730] PDF pages [22]
Recognizer outputs that have a non zero gradient are part of the correct answer, and will therefore have their value pushed down.

[P23-S00731] PDF pages [22]
The gradients present on the recognizer outputs can be backpropagated through each recognizer instance.

[P23-S00732] PDF pages [22]
For each recognizer instance, we obtain a vector of partial derivatives of the loss function with respect to the recognizer instance parameters.

[P23-S00733] PDF pages [22]
All the recognizer instances share the same parameter vector, since they are merely clones of each other, therefore the full gradient of the loss function with respect to the recognizer's parameter vector is simply the sum of the gradient vectors produced by each recognizer instance.

[P23-S00734] PDF pages [22]
Viterbi training, though formulated differently, is often use in HMM-based speech recognition systems [28].

[P23-S00735] PDF pages [22]
While it seems simple and satisfying, this training architecture has a flaw that can potentially be fatal.

[P23-S00736] PDF pages [22]
The problem was already mentioned in Section II-C.

[P23-S00737] PDF pages [22]
If the recognizer is a simple neural network with sigmoid out-put units, the minimum of the loss function is attained, not when the recognizer always gives the right answer, but when it ignores the input, and sets its output to a constant vector with small values for all the components.

[P23-S00738] PDF pages [22]
This is known as the collapse problem.

[P23-S00739] PDF pages [22]
The collapse only occurs if the recognizer outputs can simultaneously take their minimum value.

[P23-S00740] PDF pages [22]
If on the other hand the recognizer's out-put layer contains RBF units with fixed parameters, then there is no such trivial solution.

[P23-S00741] PDF pages [22]
This is due to the fact that a set of RBF with fixed distinct parameter vectors cannot simultaneously take their minimum value.

[P23-S00742] PDF pages [22]
In this case, the complete collapse described above does not occur.

[P23-S00743] PDF pages [22]
However, this does not totally prevent the occurrence of a milder collapse because the loss function still has a “flat spot” for a trivial solution with constant recognizer out-put.

[P23-S00744] PDF pages [22]
This flat spot is a saddle point, but it is attractive in almost all directions and is very difficult to get out of using gradient-based minimization procedures.

[P23-S00745] PDF pages [22]
If the parameters of the RBFs are allowed to adapt, then the collapse problems reappears because the RBF centers can all converge to a single vector, and the underlying neural network can learn to produce that vector, and ignore the input.

[P23-S00746] PDF pages [22]
A different kind of collapse occurs if the width of the RBFs are also allowed to adapt.

[P23-S00747] PDF pages [22]
The collapse only occurs if a trainable module such as a neural network feeds the RBFs.

[P23-S00748] PDF pages [22]
The collapse does not occur in HMM-based speech recognition systems because they are generative systems that produce normalized likelihoods for the input data (more on this later).

[P23-S00749] PDF pages [22]
Another way to avoid the collapse is to train the whole system with respect to a discriminative training criterion, such as maximizing the conditional probability of the correct interpretations (correct sequence of class labels) given the input image.

[P23-S00750] PDF pages [22]
Another problem with Viterbi training is that the penalty of the answer cannot be used reliably as a measure of confidence because it does not take low-penalty (or high-scoring) competing answers into account.

[P23-S00751] PDF pages [22]
A modification of the training criterion can circumvent the collapse problem described above and at the same time produce more reliable confidence values.

[P23-S00752] PDF pages [22]
The idea is to not only minimize the cumulated penalty of the lowest penalty path with the correct interpretation, but also to somehow increase the penalty of competing and possibly incorrect paths that have a dangerously low penalty.

[P23-S00753] PDF pages [22]
This type of criterion is called discriminative, because it plays the good answers against the bad ones.

[P23-S00754] PDF pages [22]
Discriminative training procedures can be seen as attempting to build appropriate separating surfaces between classes rather than to model individual classes independently of each other.

[P23-S00755] PDF pages [23]
Discriminative Viterbi Training GTN Architecture for a character string recognizer based on Heuristic Over-Segmentation.

[P23-S00756] PDF pages [23]
Quantities in square brackets are penalties computed during the forward propagation.

[P23-S00757] PDF pages [23]
Quantities in parentheses are partial derivatives computed during the backward propagation.

[P23-S00758] PDF pages [24]
This is because the conditional approach does not need to assume a particular form for the distribution of the input data.

[P23-S00759] PDF pages [24]
One example of discriminative criterion is the difference between the penalty of the Viterbi path in the constrained graph, and the penalty of the Viterbi path in the (unconstrained) interpretation graph, i.e. the difference between the penalty of the best correct path, and the penalty of the best path (correct or incorrect).

[P23-S00760] PDF pages [24]
The corresponding GTN training architecture is shown in figure 20.

[P23-S00761] PDF pages [24]
The left side of the diagram is identical to the GTN used for non-discriminative Viterbi training.

[P23-S00762] PDF pages [24]
This loss function reduces the risk of collapse because it forces the recognizer to in-creases the penalty of wrongly recognized objects.

[P23-S00763] PDF pages [24]
Discriminative training can also be seen as another example of error correction procedure, which tends to minimize the difference between the desired output computed in the left half of the GTN in figure 20 and the actual output computed in the right half of figure 20.

[P23-S00764] PDF pages [24]
Eavit is always positive since the constrained graph is a subset of the paths in the interpretation graph, and the Viterbi algorithm selects the path with the lowest total penalty.

[P23-S00765] PDF pages [24]
In the ideal case, the two paths C.yj, and Cyt coincide, and Egyit is zero.

[P23-S00766] PDF pages [24]
Back-propagating gradients through the discriminative Viterbi GTN adds some “negative” training to the pre-viously described non-discriminative training.

[P23-S00767] PDF pages [24]
The left half is identical to the non-discriminative Viterbi training GTN, therefore the back-propagation is identical.

[P23-S00768] PDF pages [24]
The gradients back-propagated through the right half of the GTN are multiplied by -1, since Cy; contributes to the loss with a negative sign.

[P23-S00769] PDF pages [24]
Otherwise the process is similar to the left half.

[P23-S00770] PDF pages [24]
The gradients on arcs of Gin, get positive contributions from the left half and negative contributions from the right half.

[P23-S00771] PDF pages [24]
The two contributions must be added, since the penalties on Gint arcs are sent to the two halves through a “Y” connection in the forward pass.

[P23-S00772] PDF pages [24]
Arcs in Gint that appear neither in Gy, nor in Gyyi, have a gradient of zero.

[P23-S00773] PDF pages [24]
They do not contribute to the cost.

[P23-S00774] PDF pages [24]
Arcs that appear in both Gyit, and Geyi, also have zero gradient.

[P23-S00775] PDF pages [24]
The -1 contribution from the right half cancels the the +1 contribution from the left half.

[P23-S00776] PDF pages [24]
In other words, when an arc is rightfully part of the answer, there is no gradient.

[P23-S00777] PDF pages [24]
If an arc appears in Gevi, but not in Gyit, the gradient is +1.

[P23-S00778] PDF pages [24]
The arc should have had a lower penalty to make it to Gyit.

[P23-S00779] PDF pages [24]
If an arc is in Gyi, but not in Geyit, the gradient is -1.

[P23-S00780] PDF pages [24]
The arc had a low penalty, but should have had a higher penalty since it is not part of the desired answer.

[P23-S00781] PDF pages [24]
Variations of this technique have been used for the speech recognition.

[P23-S00782] PDF pages [24]
Driancourt and Bottou [76] used a version of it where the loss function is saturated to a fixed value.

[P23-S00783] PDF pages [24]
This can be seen as a generalization of the Learning Vector Quantization 2 (LVQ-2) loss function [80].

[P23-S00784] PDF pages [24]
Other variations of this method use not only the Viterbi path, but the Kbest paths.

[P23-S00785] PDF pages [24]
The Discriminative Viterbi algorithm does not have the flaws of the non-discriminative version, but there are problems nonetheless.

[P23-S00786] PDF pages [24]
The main problem is that the criterion does not build a margin between the classes.

[P23-S00787] PDF pages [24]
The gradient is zero as soon as the penalty of the constrained Viterbi path is equal to that of the Viterbi path.

[P23-S00788] PDF pages [24]
It would be desirable to push up the penalties of the wrong paths when they are dangerously close to the good one.

[P23-S00789] PDF pages [24]
The following section presents a solution to this problem.

[P23-S00790] PDF pages [24]
C. Forward Scoring, and Forward Training While the penalty of the Viterbi path is perfectly appropriate for the purpose of recognition, it gives only a partial picture of the situation.

[P23-S00791] PDF pages [24]
Imagine the lowest penalty paths corresponding to several different segmentations produced the same answer (the same label sequence).

[P23-S00792] PDF pages [24]
Then it could be argued that the overall penalty for the interpretation should be smaller than the penalty obtained when only one path produced that interpretation, because multiple paths with identical label sequences are more evidence that the label sequence is correct.

[P23-S00793] PDF pages [24]
Several rules can be used compute the penalty associated to a graph that contains several parallel paths.

[P23-S00794] PDF pages [24]
We use a combination rule borrowed from a probabilistic interpretation of the penalties as negative log posteriors.

[P23-S00795] PDF pages [24]
In a probabilistic framework, the posterior probability for the interpretation should be the sum of the posteriors for all the paths that produce that interpretation.

[P23-S00796] PDF pages [24]
Translated in terms of penalties, the penalty of an interpretation should be the negative logarithm of the sum of the negative exponentials of the penalties of the individual paths.

[P23-S00797] PDF pages [24]
The overall penalty will be smaller than all the penalties of the individual paths.

[P23-S00798] PDF pages [24]
Given an interpretation, there is a well known method, called the forward algorithm for computing the above quantity efficiently [28].

[P23-S00799] PDF pages [24]
The penalty computed with this procedure for a particular interpretation is called the forward penalty.

[P23-S00800] PDF pages [24]
Consider again the concept of constrained graph, the subgraph of the interpretation graph which contains only the paths that are consistent with a particular label sequence.

[P23-S00801] PDF pages [24]
There is one constrained graph for each possible label sequence (some may be empty graphs, which have infinite penalties).

[P23-S00802] PDF pages [24]
Given an interpretation, running the forward algorithm on the corresponding constrained graph gives the forward penalty for that interpretation.

[P23-S00803] PDF pages [25]
Note that because of numerical inaccuracies, it is better to factorize the largest e~** (corresponding to the smallest penalty) out of the logarithm.

[P23-S00804] PDF pages [25]
An interesting analogy can be drawn if we consider that a graph on which we apply the forward algorithm is equivalent to a neural network on which we run a forward propagation, except that multiplications are replaced by additions, the additions are replaced by logadds, and there are no sigmoids.

[P23-S00805] PDF pages [25]
One way to understand the forward algorithm is to think about multiplicative scores (e.g., probabilities) instead of additive penalties on the arcs: score = exp(- penalty ).

[P23-S00806] PDF pages [25]
In that case the Viterbi algorithm selects the path with the largest cumulative score (with scores multiplied along the path), whereas the forward score is the sum of the cumulative scores associated to each of the possible paths from the start to the end node.

[P23-S00807] PDF pages [25]
The forward penalty is always lower than the cumulated penalty on any of the paths, but if one path “dominates” (with a much lower penalty), its penalty is almost equal to the forward penalty.

[P23-S00808] PDF pages [25]
The forward algorithm gets its name from the forward pass of the well-known Baum-Welsh algorithm for training Hidden Markov Models [28].

[P23-S00809] PDF pages [25]
Section VIII-E gives more details on the relation between this work and HMMs.

[P23-S00810] PDF pages [25]
The advantage of the forward penalty with respect to the Viterbi penalty is that it takes into account all the different ways to produce an answer, and not just the one with the lowest penalty.

[P23-S00811] PDF pages [25]
This is important if there is some ambiguity in the segmentation, since the combined forward penalty of two paths C; and C2 associated with the same label sequence may be less than the penalty of a path C3 associated with another label sequence, even though the penalty of C3 might be less than any one of C, or C2.

[P23-S00812] PDF pages [25]
The Forward training GTN is only a slight modification of the previously introduced Viterbi training GTN.

[P23-S00813] PDF pages [25]
It suffices to turn the Viterbi transformers in Figure 19 into Forward Scorers that take an interpretation graph as input an produce the forward penalty of that graph on output.

[P23-S00814] PDF pages [25]
Then the penalties of all the paths that contain the correct answer are lowered, instead of just that of the best one.

[P23-S00815] PDF pages [25]
Back-propagating through the forward penalty computation (the forward transformer) is quite different from backpropagating through a Viterbi transformer.

[P23-S00816] PDF pages [25]
All the penalties of the input graph have an influence on the forward penalty, but penalties that belong to low-penalty paths have a stronger influence.

[P23-S00817] PDF pages [25]
Discriminative Forward Training GTN Architecture for a character string recognizer based on Heuristic Over- Segmentation.

[P23-S00818] PDF pages [25]
This can be seen as a “soft” version of the back-propagation through a Viterbi scorer and transformer.

[P23-S00819] PDF pages [25]
All the arcs in G, have an influence on the loss function.

[P23-S00820] PDF pages [25]
The arcs that belong to low penalty paths have a larger influence.

[P23-S00821] PDF pages [25]
Backpropagation through the path selector is the same as before.

[P23-S00822] PDF pages [25]
The derivative with respect to Gjnt arcs that have an alter ego in G, are simply copied from the corresponding arc in G,.

[P23-S00823] PDF pages [25]
The derivatives with respect to the other arcs are 0.

[P23-S00824] PDF pages [25]
Several authors have applied the idea of backpropagating gradients through a forward scorer to train speech recognition systems, including Bridle and his a-net model [73] and Haffner and his aG-TDNN model [81], but these authors recommended discriminative training as described in the next section.

[P23-S00825] PDF pages [25]
The information contained in the forward penalty can be used in another discriminative training criterion which we will call the discriminative forward criterion.

[P23-S00826] PDF pages [25]
This criterion corresponds to maximization of the posterior probability of choosing the paths associated with the correct interpretation.

[P23-S00827] PDF pages [25]
This posterior probability is defined as the exponential of the minus the constrained forward penalty, normalized by the exponential of minus the unconstrained forward penalty.

[P23-S00828] PDF pages [25]
Note that the forward penalty of the constrained graph is always larger or equal to the forward penalty of the unconstrained interpretation graph.

[P23-S00829] PDF pages [25, 26]
Ideally, we would like the forward penalty of the constrained graph to be equal to the forward penalty of the complete interpretation graph.

[P23-S00830] PDF pages [26]
Equality between those two quantities is achieved when the combined penalties of the paths with the correct label sequence is negligibly small compared to the penalties of all the other paths, or that the posterior probability associated to the paths with the correct interpretation is almost 1, which is precisely what we want.

[P23-S00831] PDF pages [26]
The corresponding GTN training architecture is shown in figure 21.

[P23-S00832] PDF pages [26]
Eaforw is always positive since the constrained graph is a subset of the paths in the interpretation graph, and the forward penalty of a graph is always larger than the forward penalty of a subgraph of this graph.

[P23-S00833] PDF pages [26]
In the ideal case, the penalties of incorrect paths are infinitely large, therefore the two penalties coincide and Egforw is zero.

[P23-S00834] PDF pages [26]
Readers familiar with the Boltzmann machine connectionist model might recognize the constrained and unconstrained graphs as analogous to the “clamped” (constrained by the observed values of the output variable) and “free” (unconstrained) phases of the Boltzmann machine algorithm [13].

[P23-S00835] PDF pages [26]
Back-propagating derivatives through the discriminative Forward GTN distributes gradients more evenly than in the Viterbi case.

[P23-S00836] PDF pages [26]
Derivatives are back-propagated through the left half of the the GTN in Figure 21 down to the interpretation graph.

[P23-S00837] PDF pages [26]
Derivatives are negated and back-propagated through the right-half, and the result for each arc is added to the contribution from the left half.

[P23-S00838] PDF pages [26]
Each arc in Gint now has a derivative.

[P23-S00839] PDF pages [26]
Arcs that are part of a correct path have a positive derivative.

[P23-S00840] PDF pages [26]
This derivative is very large if an incorrect path has a lower penalty than all the correct paths.

[P23-S00841] PDF pages [26]
Similarly, the derivatives with respect to arcs that are part of a low-penalty incorrect path have a large negative derivative.

[P23-S00842] PDF pages [26]
On the other hand, if the penalty of a path associated with the correct interpretation is much smaller than all other paths, the loss function is very close to 0 and almost no gradient is back-propagated.

[P23-S00843] PDF pages [26]
The training therefore concentrates on examples of images which yield a classification error, and furthermore, it concentrates on the pieces of the image which cause that error.

[P23-S00844] PDF pages [26]
Discriminative forward training is an elegant and efficient way of solving the infamous credit assignment problem for learning machines that manipulate “dynamic” data structures such as graphs.

[P23-S00845] PDF pages [26]
More generally, the same idea can be used in all situations where a learning machine must choose between discrete alternative interpretations.

[P23-S00846] PDF pages [26]
As previously, the derivatives on the interpretation graph penalties can then be back-propagated into the character recognizer instances.

[P23-S00847] PDF pages [26]
Back-propagation through the character recognizer gives derivatives on its parameters.

[P23-S00848] PDF pages [26]
All the gradient contributions for the different candidate segments are added up to obtain the total gradient associated to one pair (input image, correct label sequence), that is, one example in the training set.

[P23-S00849] PDF pages [26]
A step of stochastic gradient descent can then be applied to update the parameters.

[P23-S00850] PDF pages [26]
In the above discussion, the global training criterion was given a probabilistic interpretation, but the individual penalties on the arcs of the graphs were not.

[P23-S00851] PDF pages [26]
There are good reasons for that.

[P23-S00852] PDF pages [26]
For example, if some penalties are associated to the different class labels, they would (1) have to sum to 1 (class posteriors), or (2) integrate to 1 over the input domain (likelihoods).

[P23-S00853] PDF pages [26]
Let us first discuss the first case (class posteriors normalization).

[P23-S00854] PDF pages [26]
This local normalization of penalties may eliminate information that is important for locally rejecting all the classes [82], e.g., when a piece of image does not correspond to a valid character class, because some of the segmentation candidates may be wrong.

[P23-S00855] PDF pages [26]
Although an explicit “garbage class” can be introduced in a probabilistic framework to address that question, some problems remain because it is difficult to characterize such a class probabilistically and to train a system in this way (it would require a density model of unseen or unlabeled samples).

[P23-S00856] PDF pages [26]
The probabilistic interpretation of individual variables plays an important role in the Baum-Welsh algorithm in combination with the Expectation-Maximization procedure.

[P23-S00857] PDF pages [26]
Unfortunately, those methods cannot be applied to discriminative training criteria, and one is reduced to using gradient-based methods.

[P23-S00858] PDF pages [26]
Enforcing the normalization of the probabilistic quantities while performing gradientbased learning is complex, inefficient, time consuming, and creates ill-conditioning of the loss-function.

[P23-S00859] PDF pages [26]
Following [82], we therefore prefer to postpone normalization as far as possible (in fact, until the final decision stage of the system).

[P23-S00860] PDF pages [26]
Without normalization, the quantities manipulated in the system do not have a direct probabilistic interpretation.

[P23-S00861] PDF pages [26]
Let us now discuss the second case (using a generative model of the input).

[P23-S00862] PDF pages [26]
Generative models build the boundary indirectly, by first building an independent density model for each class, and then performing classification decisions on the basis of these models.

[P23-S00863] PDF pages [26]
This is not a discriminative approach in that it does not focus on the ultimate goal of learning, which in this case is to learn the classification decision surface.

[P23-S00864] PDF pages [26]
Theoretical arguments [6], [7] suggest that estimating input densities when the real goal is to obtain a discriminant function for classification is a suboptimal strategy.

[P23-S00865] PDF pages [26]
In theory, the problem of estimating densities in high-dimensional spaces is much more ill-posed than finding decision boundaries.

[P23-S00866] PDF pages [26]
Even though the internal variables of the system do not have a direct probabilistic interpretation, the overall system can still be viewed as producing posterior probabilities for the classes.

[P23-S00867] PDF pages [26]
In fact, assuming that a particular label sequence is given as the “desired sequence” to the GTN in figure 21, the exponential of minus Eaforw can be interpreted as an estimate of the posterior probability of that label sequence given the input.

[P23-S00868] PDF pages [26]
The sum of those posteriors for all the possible label sequences is 1.

[P23-S00869] PDF pages [26]
Another approach would consists of directly minimizing an approximation of the number of misclassifications [83] [76].

[P23-S00870] PDF pages [27]
Explicit segmentation can be avoided by sweeping a recognizer at every possible location in the input field.

[P23-S00871] PDF pages [27]
We will see in Section X-C that this is a good way to obtain scores on which to base a rejection strategy.

[P23-S00872] PDF pages [27]
The important point being made here is that one is free to choose any parameterization deemed appropriate for a classification model.

[P23-S00873] PDF pages [27]
The fact that a particular parameterization uses internal variables with no clear probabilistic interpretation does not make the model any less legitimate than models that manipulate normalized quantities.

[P23-S00874] PDF pages [27]
An important advantage of global and discriminative training is that learning focuses on the most important errors, and the system learns to integrate the ambiguities from the segmentation algorithm with the ambiguities of the character recognizer.

[P23-S00875] PDF pages [27]
In Section IX we present experimental results with an on-line handwriting recognition system that confirm the advantages of using global training versus separate training.

[P23-S00876] PDF pages [27]
MULTIPLE OBJECT RECOGNITION: SPACE DISPLACEMENT NEURAL NETWORK There is a simple alternative to explicitly segmenting images of character strings using heuristics.

[P23-S00877] PDF pages [27]
The idea is to sweep a recognizer at all possible locations across a normalized image of the entire word or string as shown in Figure 22.

[P23-S00878] PDF pages [27]
With this technique, no segmentation heuristics are required since the system essentially examines all the possible segmentations of the input.

[P23-S00879] PDF pages [27]
However, there are problems with this approach.

[P23-S00880] PDF pages [27]
First, the method is in general quite expensive.

[P23-S00881] PDF pages [27]
The recognizer must be applied at every possible location on the input, or at least at a large enough subset of locations so that misalignments of characters in the field of view of the recognizers are small enough to have no effect on the error rate.

[P23-S00882] PDF pages [27]
Second, when the recognizer is centered on a character to be recognized, the neighbors of the center character will be present in the field of view of the recognizer, possibly touching the center character.

[P23-S00883] PDF pages [27]
Therefore the recognizer must be able to correctly recognize the character in the center of its input field, even if neighboring characters are very close to, or touching the central character.

[P23-S00884] PDF pages [27]
Third, a word or character string cannot be perfectly size normalized.

[P23-S00885] PDF pages [27]
A Space Displacement Neural Network is a convolutional network that has been replicated over a wide input field.

[P23-S00886] PDF pages [27]
Therefore the recognizer must be very robust to shifts and size variations.

[P23-S00887] PDF pages [27]
These three problems are elegantly circumvented if a convolutional network is replicated over the input field.

[P23-S00888] PDF pages [27]
First of all, as shown in section III, convolutional neural networks are very robust to shifts and scale variations of the input image, as well as to noise and extraneous marks in the input.

[P23-S00889] PDF pages [27]
These properties take care of the latter two problems mentioned in the previous paragraph.

[P23-S00890] PDF pages [27]
Second, convolutional networks provide a drastic saving in computational requirement when replicated over large input fields.

[P23-S00891] PDF pages [27]
A replicated convolutional network, also called a Space Displacement Neural Network or SDNN [27], is shown in Figure 23.

[P23-S00892] PDF pages [27]
While scanning a recognizer can be prohibitively expensive in general, convolutional networks can be scanned or replicated very efficiently over large, variable-size input fields.

[P23-S00893] PDF pages [27]
Consider one instance of a convolutional net and its alter ego at a nearby location.

[P23-S00894] PDF pages [27]
Because of the convolutional nature of the network, units in the two instances that look at identical locations on the input have identical outputs, therefore their states do not need to be computed twice.

[P23-S00895] PDF pages [27]
Only a thin “slice” of new states that are not shared by the two network instances needs to be recomputed.

[P23-S00896] PDF pages [27]
When all the slices are put to-gether, the result is simply a larger convolutional network whose structure is identical to the original network, except that the feature maps are larger in the horizontal dimension.

[P23-S00897] PDF pages [27]
In other words, replicating a convolutional network can be done simply by increasing the size of the fields over which the convolutions are performed, and by replicating the output layer accordingly.

[P23-S00898] PDF pages [27]
The output layer effectively becomes a convolutional layer.

[P23-S00899] PDF pages [27]
An output whose receptive field is centered on an elementary object will produce the class of this object, while an in-between output may indicate no character or contain rubbish.

[P23-S00900] PDF pages [27]
The outputs can be interpreted as evidences for the presence of objects at all possible positions in the input field.

[P23-S00901] PDF pages [28]
Although the idea of SDNN is quite old, and very attractive by its simplicity, it has not generated wide interest until recently because, as stated above, it puts enormous demands on the recognizer [26], [27].

[P23-S00902] PDF pages [28]
In speech recognition, where the recognizer is at least one order of magnitude smaller, replicated convolutional networks are easier to implement, for instance in Haffner's Multi-State TDNN model [78], [85].

[P23-S00903] PDF pages [28]
A. Interpreting the Output of an SDNN with a GTN The output of an SDNN is a sequence of vectors which encode the likelihoods, penalties, or scores of finding character of a particular class label at the corresponding location in the input.

[P23-S00904] PDF pages [28]
A post-processor is required to pull out the best possible label sequence from this vector sequence.

[P23-S00905] PDF pages [28]
An example of SDNN output is shown in Figure 25.

[P23-S00906] PDF pages [28]
Very often, individual characters are spotted by several neighboring instances of the recognizer, a consequence of the robustness of the recognizer to horizontal translations.

[P23-S00907] PDF pages [28]
Also quite often, characters are erroneously detected by recognizer instances that see only a piece of a character.

[P23-S00908] PDF pages [28]
For example a recognizer instance that only sees the right third of a “4” might output the label 1.

[P23-S00909] PDF pages [28]
How can we eliminate those extraneous characters from the out-put sequence and pull-out the best interpretation?

[P23-S00910] PDF pages [28]
This can be done using a new type of Graph Transformer with two input graphs as shown in Figure 24.

[P23-S00911] PDF pages [28]
The sequence of vectors produced by the SDNN is first coded into a linear graph with multiple arcs between pairs of successive nodes.

[P23-S00912] PDF pages [28]
Each arc between a particular pair of nodes contains the label of one of the possible categories, together with the penalty produced by the SDNN for that class label at that location.

[P23-S00913] PDF pages [28]
This graph is called the SDNN Output Graph.

[P23-S00914] PDF pages [28]
The second input graph to the transformer is a grammar transducer, more specifically a finite-state transducer [86], that encodes the relationship between input strings of class labels and corresponding output strings of recognized characters.The transducer is a weighted finite state machine (a graph) where each arc contains a pair of labels and possibly a penalty.

[P23-S00915] PDF pages [28]
Like a finite-state machine, a transducer is in a state and follows an arc to a new state when an observed input symbol matches the first symbol in the symbol pair attached to the arc.

[P23-S00916] PDF pages [28]
At this point the transducer emits the second symbol in the pair together with a penalty that combines the penalty of the input symbol and the penalty of the arc.

[P23-S00917] PDF pages [28]
A transducer therefore transforms a weighted symbol sequence into another weighted symbol sequence.

[P23-S00918] PDF pages [28]
The graph transformer shown in figure 24 performs a composition between the recognition graph and the grammar transducer.

[P23-S00919] PDF pages [28]
This operation takes every possible sequence corresponding to every possible path in the recognition graph and matches them with the paths in the grammar transducer.

[P23-S00920] PDF pages [28]
The composition produces the interpretation graph, which contains a path for each corresponding output label sequence.

[P23-S00921] PDF pages [28]
This composition operation may seem combinatorially intractable, but it turns out there exists an efficient algorithm for it described in more details in Section VIII.

[P23-S00922] PDF pages [28]
A Graph Transformer pulls out the best interpretation from the output of the SDNN.

[P23-S00923] PDF pages [28]
An example of multiple character recognition with SDNN.

[P23-S00924] PDF pages [28]
With SDNN, no explicit segmentation is performed.

[P23-S00925] PDF pages [28]
In a series of experiments, LeNet-5 was trained with the goal of being replicated so as to recognize multiple characters without segmentations.

[P23-S00926] PDF pages [28]
The data was generated from the previously described Modified NIST set as follows.

[P23-S00927] PDF pages [28]
Training images were composed of a central character, flanked by two side characters picked at random in the training set.

[P23-S00928] PDF pages [28]
The separation between the bounding boxes of the characters were chosen at random between -1 and 4 pixels.

[P23-S00929] PDF pages [28]
In other instances, no central character was present, in which case the desired output of the network was the blank space class.

[P23-S00930] PDF pages [28]
In addition, training images were degraded with 10% salt and pepper noise (random pixel inversions).

[P23-S00931] PDF pages [28]
Figures 25 and 26 show a few examples of successful recognitions of multiple characters by the LeNet-5 SDNN.

[P23-S00932] PDF pages [28]
Standard techniques based on Heuristic Over- Segmentation would fail miserably on many of those examples.

[P23-S00933] PDF pages [28]
As can be seen on these examples, the network exhibits striking invariance and noise resistance properties.

[P23-S00934] PDF pages [28]
While some authors have argued that invariance requires more sophisticated models than feed-forward neural networks [87], LeNet-5 exhibits these properties to a large extent.

[P23-S00935] PDF pages [29]
An SDNN applied to a noisy image of digit string.

[P23-S00936] PDF pages [29]
The digits shown in the SDNN output represent the winning class labels, with a lighter grey level for high-penalty answers.

[P23-S00937] PDF pages [29]
Similarly, it has been suggested that accurate recognition of multiple overlapping objects require explicit mechanisms that would solve the so-called feature binding problem [87].

[P23-S00938] PDF pages [29]
As can be seen on Figures 25 and 26, the network is able to tell the characters apart, even when they are closely intertwined, a task that would be impossible to achieve with the more classical Heuristic Over-Segmentation technique.

[P23-S00939] PDF pages [29]
The SDNN is also able to correctly group disconnected pieces of ink that form characters.

[P23-S00940] PDF pages [29]
Good examples of that are shown in the upper half of figure 26.

[P23-S00941] PDF pages [29]
In the top left example, the 4 and the 0 are more connected to each other than they are connected with themselves, yet the system correctly identifies the 4 and the 0 as separate objects.

[P23-S00942] PDF pages [29]
The top right example is interesting for several reasons.

[P23-S00943] PDF pages [29]
First the system correctly identifies the three individual ones.

[P23-S00944] PDF pages [29]
Second, the left half and right half of disconnected 4 are correctly grouped, even though no geometrical information could decide to associate the left half to the vertical bar on its left or on its right.

[P23-S00945] PDF pages [29]
The right half of the 4 does cause the appearance of an erroneous 1 on the SDNN output, but this one is removed by the character model transducer which prevents characters from appearing on contiguous outputs.

[P23-S00946] PDF pages [29]
Specialized analog/digital chips have been designed and used in character recognition, and in image preprocessing applications [88].

[P23-S00947] PDF pages [29]
However the rapid progress of conventional processor technology with reduced-precision vector arithmetic instructions (such as Intel's MMX) make the success of specialized hardware hypothetical at best.

[P23-S00948] PDF pages [29]
In the above experiments, the string image were artificially generated from individual character.

[P23-S00949] PDF pages [29]
The advantage is that we know in advance the location and the label of the important character.

[P23-S00950] PDF pages [29]
With real training data, the correct sequence of labels for a string is generally available, but the precise locations of each corresponding character in the input image are unknown.

[P23-S00951] PDF pages [29]
In the experiments described in the previous section, the best interpretation was extracted from the SDNN output using a very simple graph transformer.

[P23-S00952] PDF pages [29]
Global training of an SDNN can be performed by back-propagating gradients through such graph transformers arranged in architectures similar to the ones described in section VI.

[P23-S00953] PDF pages [30]
A globally trainable SDNN/HMM hybrid system expressed asa GTN.

[P23-S00954] PDF pages [30]
This is somewhat equivalent to modeling the output of an SDNN with a Hidden Markov Model.

[P23-S00955] PDF pages [30]
Space Displacement Neural Networks have been used in combination with HMMs or other elastic matching methods for handwritten word recognition [91], [92].

[P23-S00956] PDF pages [30]
The top part is comparable to the top part of figure 21.

[P23-S00957] PDF pages [30]
On the right side the composition of the recognition graph with the grammar gives the interpretation graph with all the possible legal interpretations.

[P23-S00958] PDF pages [30]
On the left side the composition is performed with a grammar that only contains paths with the desired sequence of labels.

[P23-S00959] PDF pages [30]
This has a somewhat similar function to the path selector used in the previous section.

[P23-S00960] PDF pages [30]
Like in Section VI-D the loss function is the difference between the forward score obtained from the left half and the forward score obtained from the right half.

[P23-S00961] PDF pages [30]
To back-propagate through the composition transformer, we need to keep a record of which arc in the recognition graph originated which arcs in the interpretation graph.

[P23-S00962] PDF pages [30]
The derivative with respect to an arc in the recognition graph is equal to the sum of the derivatives with respect to all the arcs in the interpretation graph that originated from it.

[P23-S00963] PDF pages [30]
Derivative can also be computed for the penalties on the grammar graph, allowing to learn them as well.

[P23-S00964] PDF pages [30]
As in the previous example, a discriminative criterion must be used, because using a non-discriminative criterion could result in a collapse effect if the network's output RBF are adaptive.

[P23-S00965] PDF pages [30]
The above training procedure can be equivalently formulated in term of HMM.

[P23-S00966] PDF pages [30]
Early experiments in zip code recognition [91], and more recent experiments in on-line handwriting recognition [38] have demonstrated the idea of globally-trained SDNN/HMM hybrids.

[P23-S00967] PDF pages [30]
SDNN is an extremely promising and attractive technique for OCR, but so far it has not yielded better results than Heuristic Over- Segmentation.

[P23-S00968] PDF pages [30]
We hope that these results will improve as more experience is gained with these models.

[P23-S00969] PDF pages [30]
D. Object Detection and Spotting with SDNN An interesting application of SDNNs is object detection and spotting.

[P23-S00970] PDF pages [30]
The invariance properties of Convolutional Networks, combined with the efficiency with which they can be replicated over large fields suggest that they can be used for “brute force” object spotting and detection in large images.

[P23-S00971] PDF pages [30]
The main idea is to train a single Convolutional Network to distinguish images of the object of interest from images present in the background.

[P23-S00972] PDF pages [30]
In utilization mode, the network is replicated so as to cover the entire image to be analyzed, thereby forming a two-dimensional Space Displacement Neural Network.

[P23-S00973] PDF pages [30]
The output of the SDNN is a two-dimensional plane in which activated units indicate the presence of the object of interest in the corresponding receptive field.

[P23-S00974] PDF pages [30]
Since the sizes of the objects to be detected within the image are unknown, the image can be presented to the network at multiple resolutions, and the results at multiple resolutions combined.

[P23-S00975] PDF pages [30]
The idea has been applied to face location, [93], address block location on envelopes [94], and hand tracking in video [95].

[P23-S00976] PDF pages [30]
To illustrate the method, we will consider the case of face detection in images as described in [93].

[P23-S00977] PDF pages [30]
First, images containing faces at various scales are collected.

[P23-S00978] PDF pages [30]
Those images are filtered through a zero-mean Laplacian filter so as to remove variations in global illumination and low spatial frequency illumination gradients.

[P23-S00979] PDF pages [30]
Then, training samples of faces and non-faces are manually extracted from those images.

[P23-S00980] PDF pages [30]
The face sub-images are then size normalized so that the height of the entire face is approximately 20 pixels while keeping fairly large variations (within a factor of two).

[P23-S00981] PDF pages [30]
The scale of background sub-images are picked at random.

[P23-S00982] PDF pages [30]
A single convolutional network is trained on those samples to classify face sub-images from non-face sub-images.

[P23-S00983] PDF pages [30]
When a scene image is to be analyzed, it is first filtered through the Laplacian filter, and sub-sampled at powersof-two resolutions.

[P23-S00984] PDF pages [30]
The network is replicated over each of multiple resolution images.

[P23-S00985] PDF pages [30]
A simple voting technique is used to combine the results from multiple resolutions.

[P23-S00986] PDF pages [30]
A two-dimensional version of the global training method described in the previous section can be used to alleviate the need to manually locate faces when building the training sample [93].

[P23-S00987] PDF pages [30]
Each possible location is seen as an alternative interpretation, i.e. one of several parallel arcs in a simple graph that only contains a start node and an end node.

[P23-S00988] PDF pages [30]
Other authors have used Neural Networks, or other classifiers such as Support Vector Machines for face detection with great success [96], [97].

[P23-S00989] PDF pages [30]
Their systems are very similar to the one described above, including the idea of presenting the image to the network at multiple scales.

[P23-S00990] PDF pages [30, 31]
But since those systems do not use Convolutional Networks, they cannot take advantage of the speedup described here, and have to rely on other techniques, such as pre-filtering and real-time tracking, to keep the computational requirement within reasonable limits.

[P23-S00991] PDF pages [31]
In addition, because those classifiers are much less invariant to scale variations than Convolutional Networks, it is necessary to multiply the number of scales at which the images are presented to the classifier.

[P23-S00992] PDF pages [31]
In Section IV, Graph Transformer Networks (GTN) were introduced as a generalization of multi-layer, multi-module networks where the state information is represented as graphs instead of fixed-size vectors.

[P23-S00993] PDF pages [31]
This section re-interprets the GTNs in the framework of Generalized Transduction, and proposes a powerful Graph Composition algorithm.

[P23-S00994] PDF pages [31]
Similar ideas have been applied to handwriting recognition (see [38] for a re-view).

[P23-S00995] PDF pages [31]
However, there has been no proposal for a systematic approach to multi-layer graph-based trainable systems.

[P23-S00996] PDF pages [31]
The idea of transforming graphs into other graphs has re-ceived considerable interest in computer science, through the concept of weighted finite-state transducers [86].

[P23-S00997] PDF pages [31]
Transducers have been applied to speech recognition [100] and language translation [101], and proposals have been made for handwriting recognition [102].

[P23-S00998] PDF pages [31]
This line of work has been mainly focused on efficient search algorithms [103] and on the algebraic aspects of combining transducers and graphs (called acceptors in this context), but very little effort has been devoted to building globally trainable systems out of transducers.

[P23-S00999] PDF pages [31]
What is proposed in the following sections is a systematic approach to automatic training in graph-manipulating systems.

[P23-S01000] PDF pages [31]
A different approach to graph-based trainable systems, called Input-Output HMM, was proposed in [104], [105].

[P23-S01001] PDF pages [31]
In the established framework of finite-state transducers [86], discrete symbols are attached to arcs in the graphs.

[P23-S01002] PDF pages [31]
Acceptor graphs have a single symbol attached to each arc whereas transducer graphs have two symbols (an input symbol and an output symbol).

[P23-S01003] PDF pages [31]
A special null symbol is absorbed by any other symbol (when concatenating symbols to build a symbol sequence).

[P23-S01004] PDF pages [31]
Weighted transducers and acceptors also have a scalar quantity attached to each arc.

[P23-S01005] PDF pages [31]
In this framework, the composition operation takes as input an acceptor graph and a transducer graph and builds an output acceptor graph.

[P23-S01006] PDF pages [31]
Each path in this output graph (with symbol sequence Soyt) corresponds to one path (with symbol sequence Sin) in the input acceptor graph and one path and a corresponding pair of input/output sequences (Sout,Sin) in the transducer graph.

[P23-S01007] PDF pages [31]
The weights on the arcs of the output graph are obtained by adding the weights from the matching arcs in the input acceptor and transducer graphs.

[P23-S01008] PDF pages [31]
In the rest of the paper, we will call this graph composition operation using transducers the (standard) transduction operation.

[P23-S01009] PDF pages [31]
A simple example of transduction is shown in Figure 28.

[P23-S01010] PDF pages [31]
In this simple example, the input and output symbols on the transducer arcs are always identical.

[P23-S01011] PDF pages [31]
This type of transducer graph is called a grammar graph.

[P23-S01012] PDF pages [31]
To better understand the transduction operation, imagine two tokens sitting each on the start nodes of the input acceptor graph and the transducer graph.

[P23-S01013] PDF pages [31]
The tokens can freely follow any arc labeled with a null input symbol.

[P23-S01014] PDF pages [31]
A token can follow an arc labeled with a non-null input symbol if the other token also follows an arc labeled with the same in-put symbol.

[P23-S01015] PDF pages [31]
We have an acceptable trajectory when both tokens reach the end nodes of their graphs (i.e. the tokens have reached the terminal configuration).

[P23-S01016] PDF pages [31]
This trajectory represents a sequence of input symbols that complies with both the acceptor and the transducer.

[P23-S01017] PDF pages [31]
We can then collect the corresponding sequence of output symbols along the trajectory of the transducer token.

[P23-S01018] PDF pages [31]
The above procedure produces a tree, but a simple technique described in Section VITI-C can be used to avoid generating multiple copies of certain subgraphs by detecting when a particular output state has already been seen.

[P23-S01019] PDF pages [31]
The transduction operation can be performed very efficiently [106], but presents complex book-keeping problems concerning the handling of all combinations of null and non null symbols.

[P23-S01020] PDF pages [31]
If the weights are interpreted as probabilities (normalized appropriately) then an acceptor graph represents a probability distribution over the language defined by the set of label sequences associated to all possible paths (from the start to the end node) in the graph.

[P23-S01021] PDF pages [31]
An example of application of the transduction operation is the incorporation of linguistic constraints (a lexicon or a grammar) when recognizing words or other character strings.

[P23-S01022] PDF pages [31]
The recognition transformer produces the recognition graph (an acceptor graph) by applying the neural network recognizer to each candidate segment.

[P23-S01023] PDF pages [31]
This acceptor graph is composed with a transducer graph for the grammar.

[P23-S01024] PDF pages [31]
The grammar transducer contains a path for each legal sequence of symbol, possibly augmented with penalties to indicate the relative likelihoods of the possible sequences.

[P23-S01025] PDF pages [31]
The arcs contain identical input and output symbols.

[P23-S01026] PDF pages [31]
Another example of transduction was mentioned in Section V: the path selector used in the heuristic oversegmentation training GTN is implementable by a composition.

[P23-S01027] PDF pages [31]
The transducer graph is linear graph which contains the correct label sequence.

[P23-S01028] PDF pages [31]
The composition of the interpretation graph with this linear graph yields the constrained graph.

[P23-S01029] PDF pages [31, 32]
