If the data structures associated to each arc took only a finite number of values, composing the input graph and an appropriate transducer would be a sound solution.

[P23-S01030] PDF pages [32]
For our applications however, the data structures attached to the arcs of the graphs may be vectors, images or other high-dimensional objects that are not readily enumerated.

[P23-S01031] PDF pages [32]
We present a new composition operation that solves this problem.

[P23-S01032] PDF pages [32]
Instead of only handling graphs with discrete symbols and penalties on the arcs, we are interested in considering graphs whose arcs may carry complex data structures, in-cluding continuous-valued data structures such as vectors and images.

[P23-S01033] PDF pages [32]
Composing such graphs requires additional information: e When examining a pair of arcs (one from each input graph), we need a criterion to decide whether to create corresponding arc(s) and node(s) in the output graph, based on the information attached to the input arcs.

[P23-S01034] PDF pages [32]
We can decide to build an arc, several arcs, or an entire sub-graph with several nodes and arcs.

[P23-S01035] PDF pages [32]
These functions are encapsulated in an object called a Composition Transformer.

[P23-S01036] PDF pages [32]
An instance of Composition Transformer implements three methods: e check(arcl, arc2) compares the data structures pointed to by arcs arci (from the first graph) and arc2 (from the second graph) and re-turns a boolean indicating whether corresponding arc(s) should be created in the output graph.

[P23-S01037] PDF pages [32]
This assumes that the function used by fprop to compute the values attached to its output arcs is differentiable.

[P23-S01038] PDF pages [32]
The check method can be seen as constructing a dynamic architecture of functional dependencies, while the fprop method performs a forward propagation through that architecture to compute the numerical information attached to the arcs.

[P23-S01039] PDF pages [32]
The bprop method performs a backward propagation through the same architecture to compute the partial derivatives of the loss function with respect to the information attached to the arcs.

[P23-S01040] PDF pages [32]
Example of composition of the recognition graph with the grammar graph in order to build an interpretation that is consistent with both of them.

[P23-S01041] PDF pages [32]
During the forward propagation (dark arrows), the methods check and fprop are used.

[P23-S01042] PDF pages [32]
Gradients (dashed arrows) are back-propagated with the application of the method bprop.

[P23-S01043] PDF pages [32]
The management of null transitions is a straightforward modification of the token simulation function.

[P23-S01044] PDF pages [32]
Before enumerating the possible non null joint token transitions, we loop on the possible null transitions of each token, recursively call the token simulation function, and finally call the method fprop.

[P23-S01045] PDF pages [32]
The safest way for identifying acceptable trajectories consists in running a preliminary pass for identifying the token configurations from which we can reach the terminal configuration (i.e. both tokens on the end nodes).

[P23-S01046] PDF pages [32]
This is easily achieved by enumerating the trajectories in the opposite direction.

[P23-S01047] PDF pages [32]
We start on the end nodes and follow the arcs upstream.

[P23-S01048] PDF pages [32]
During the main pass, we only build the nodes that allow the tokens to reach the terminal configuration.

[P23-S01049] PDF pages [32]
Graph composition using transducers (i.e. standard transduction) is easily and efficiently implemented as a generalized transduction.

[P23-S01050] PDF pages [32]
The method check simply tests the equality of the input symbols on the two arcs, and the method fprop creates a single arc whose symbol is the output symbol on the transducer's arc.

[P23-S01051] PDF pages [32]
The composition between pairs of graphs is particularly useful for incorporating linguistic constraints in a handwriting recognizer.

[P23-S01052] PDF pages [32]
Examples of its use are given in the on-line handwriting recognition system described in Section IX) and in the check reading system described in Section X).

[P23-S01053] PDF pages [32]
In the rest of the paper, the term Composition Transformer will denote a Graph Transformer based on the generalized transductions of multiple graphs.

[P23-S01054] PDF pages [32]
The concept of generalized transduction is a very general one.

[P23-S01055] PDF pages [33]
Pseudo-code for a simplified generalized composition algorithm.

[P23-S01056] PDF pages [33]
For simplifying the presentation, we do not handle null transitions nor implement dead end avoidance.

[P23-S01057] PDF pages [33]
The two main component of the composition appear clearly here: (a) the re-cursive function simtoken() enumerating the token trajectories, and, (b) the associative array map used for remembering which nodes of the composed graph have been visited.

[P23-S01058] PDF pages [33]
In this case the, the generalized transduction does not take two in-put graphs but a single input graph.

[P23-S01059] PDF pages [33]
The method fprop of the transformer may create several arcs or even a complete subgraph for each arc of the initial graph.

[P23-S01060] PDF pages [33]
In fact the pair (check, fprop) itself can be seen as procedurally defining a transducer.

[P23-S01061] PDF pages [33]
In addition, It can be shown that the generalized transduction of a single graph is theoretically equivalent to the standard composition of this graph with a particular transducer graph.

[P23-S01062] PDF pages [33]
However, implementing the operation this way may be very inefficient since the transducer can be very complicated.

[P23-S01063] PDF pages [33]
In practice, the graph produced by a generalized transduction is represented procedurally, in order to avoid building the whole output graph (which may be huge when for example the interpretation graph is composed with the grammar graph).

[P23-S01064] PDF pages [33]
We only instantiate the nodes which are visited by the search algorithm during recognition (e.g. Viterbi).

[P23-S01065] PDF pages [33]
This strategy propagates the benefits of pruning algorithms (e.g. Beam Search) in all the Graph Transformer Network.

[P23-S01066] PDF pages [33]
D. Notes on the Graph Structures Section VI has discussed the idea of global training by back-propagating gradient through simple graph transformers.

[P23-S01067] PDF pages [33]
The bprop method is the basis of the backpropagation algorithm for generic graph transformers.

[P23-S01068] PDF pages [33]
A generalized composition transformer can be seen as dynamically establishing functional relationships between the numerical quantities on the input and output arcs.

[P23-S01069] PDF pages [33]
Once the check function has decided that a relationship should be established, the fprop function implements the numerical re-lationship.

[P23-S01070] PDF pages [33]
The check function establishes the structure of the ephemeral network inside the composition transformer.

[P23-S01071] PDF pages [33]
Since fprop is assumed to be differentiable, gradients can be back-propagated through that structure.

[P23-S01072] PDF pages [33]
Most parameters affect the scores stored on the arcs of the successive graphs of the system.

[P23-S01073] PDF pages [33]
A few threshold parameters may determine whether an arc appears or not in the graph.

[P23-S01074] PDF pages [33]
Since non existing arcs are equivalent to arcs with very large penalties, we only consider the case of parameters affecting the penalties.

[P23-S01075] PDF pages [33]
In the kind of systems we have discussed until now (and the application described in Section X), much of the knowledge about the structure of the graph that is produced by a Graph Transformer is determined by the nature of the Graph Transformer, but it may also depend on the value of the parameters and on the input.

[P23-S01076] PDF pages [33]
It may also be interesting to consider Graph Transformer modules which attempt to learn the structure of the output graph.

[P23-S01077] PDF pages [33]
This might be considered a combinatorial problem and not amenable to Gradient-Based Learning, but a solution to this problem is to generate a large graph that contains the graph candidates as sub-graphs, and then select the appropriate sub-graph.

[P23-S01078] PDF pages [34]
E. GTN and Hidden Markov Models GTNs can be seen as a generalization and an extension of HMMs.

[P23-S01079] PDF pages [34]
On the one hand, the probabilistic interpretation can be either kept (with penalties being log-probabilities), pushed to the final decision stage (with the difference of the constrained forward penalty and the unconstrained forward penalty being interpreted as negative log-probabilities of label sequences), or dropped altogether (the network just represents a decision surface for label sequences in input space).

[P23-S01080] PDF pages [34]
On the other hand, Graph Transformer Networks extend HMMs by allowing to combine in a well-principled framework multiple levels of processing, or multiple models (e.g., Pereira et al. have been using the transducer framework for stacking HMMs representing different levels of processing in automatic speech recognition [86]).

[P23-S01081] PDF pages [34]
Unfolding a HMM in time yields a graph that is very similar to our interpretation graph (at the final stage of processing of the Graph Transformer Network, before Viterbi recognition).

[P23-S01082] PDF pages [34]
It has nodes n(t,i) associated to each time step ¢ and state 7 in the model.

[P23-S01083] PDF pages [34]
The penalty c; for an arc from n(t - 1,7) to n(t,7) then corresponds to the negative log-probability of emitting observed data o; at position ¢ and going from state j to state i in the time interval (t -1,t).

[P23-S01084] PDF pages [34]
With this probabilistic interpretation, the forward penalty is the negative logarithm of the likelihood of whole observed data sequence (given the model).

[P23-S01085] PDF pages [34]
In Section VI we mentioned that the collapsing phenomenon can occur when non-discriminative loss functions are used to train neural networks/HMM hybrid systems.

[P23-S01086] PDF pages [34]
With classical HMMs with fixed preprocessing, this problem does not occur because the parameters of the emission and transition probability models are forced to satisfy certain probabilistic constraints: the sum or the integral of the probabilities of a random variable over its possible values must be 1.

[P23-S01087] PDF pages [34]
Therefore, when the probability of certain events is increased, the probability of other events must automatically be decreased.

[P23-S01088] PDF pages [34]
The Input-Output HMM model (IOHMM) [105], [109], is strongly related to graph transformers.

[P23-S01089] PDF pages [34]
Viewed as a probabilistic model, an IOHMM represents the conditional distribution of output sequences given input sequences (of the same or a different length).

[P23-S01090] PDF pages [34]
It is parameterized from an emission probability module and a transition probability module.

[P23-S01091] PDF pages [34]
The emission probability module computes the conditional emission probability of an output variable (given an input value and the value of discrete “state” variable).

[P23-S01092] PDF pages [34]
The transition probability module computes conditional transition probabilities of a change in the value of the “state” variable, given the an input value.

[P23-S01093] PDF pages [34]
Viewed as a graph transformer, it assigns an output graph (representing a probability distribution over the sequences of the output variable) to each path in the input graph.

[P23-S01094] PDF pages [34]
The input values of the emission and transition modules are read off the data structure on the input arcs of the IOHMM Graph Transformer.

[P23-S01095] PDF pages [34]
In practice, the out-put graph may be very large, and needs not be completely instantiated (i.e., it is pruned: only the low penalty paths are created).

[P23-S01096] PDF pages [34]
AN ON-LINE HANDWRITING RECOGNITION SYSTEM Natural handwriting is often a mixture of different “styles”, lower case printed, upper case, and cursive.

[P23-S01097] PDF pages [34]
A reliable recognizer for such handwriting would greatly improve interaction with pen-based devices, but its implementation presents new technical challenges.

[P23-S01098] PDF pages [34]
Characters taken in isolation can be very ambiguous, but considerable information is available from the context of the whole word.

[P23-S01099] PDF pages [34]
We have built a word recognition system for penbased devices based on four main modules: a preprocessor that normalizes a word, or word group, by fitting a geometrical model to the word structure; a module that produces an “annotated image” from the normalized pen trajectory; a replicated convolutional neural network that spots and recognizes characters; and a GTN that interprets the networks output by taking word-level constraints into account.

[P23-S01100] PDF pages [34]
The network and the GTN are jointly trained to minimize an error measure defined at the word level.

[P23-S01101] PDF pages [34]
In this work, we have compared a system based on SDNNs (such as described in Section VII), and a system based on Heuristic Over-Segmentation (such as described in Section V).

[P23-S01102] PDF pages [34]
Because of the sequential nature of the information in the pen trajectory (which reveals more information than the purely optical input from in image), Heuristic Over-Segmentation can be very efficient in proposing candidate character cuts, especially for non-cursive script.

[P23-S01103] PDF pages [34]
Input normalization reduces intra-character variability, simplifying character recognition.

[P23-S01104] PDF pages [34]
We have used a word normalization scheme [92] based on fitting a geometrical model of the word structure.

[P23-S01105] PDF pages [34]
Our model has four “flexible” lines representing respectively the ascenders line, the core line, the base line and the descenders line.

[P23-S01106] PDF pages [34]
The lines are fitted to local minima or maxima of the pen trajectory.

[P23-S01107] PDF pages [34]
The parameters of the lines are estimated with a modified version of the EM algorithm to maximize the joint probability of observed points and parameter values, using a prior on parameters that prevents the lines from collapsing on each other.

[P23-S01108] PDF pages [34]
The recognition of handwritten characters from a pen trajectory on a digitizing surface is often done in the time domain [110], [44], [111].

[P23-S01109] PDF pages [34]
Typically, trajectories are normalized, and local geometrical or dynamical features are extracted.

[P23-S01110] PDF pages [34]
The recognition may then be performed using curve matching [110], or other classification techniques such as TDNNs [44], [111].

[P23-S01111] PDF pages [35]
An on-line handwriting recognition GTN based on heuristic over-segmentation use in high accuracy, writer independent systems that in-tegrate the segmentation with the recognition.

[P23-S01112] PDF pages [35]
Since the intent of the writer is to produce a legible image, it seems natural to preserve as much of the pictorial nature of the signal as possible, while at the same time exploit the sequential information in the trajectory.

[P23-S01113] PDF pages [35]
For this purpose we have designed a representation scheme, called AMAP [38], where pen trajectories are represented by low-resolution images in which each picture element contains information about the local properties of the trajectory.

[P23-S01114] PDF pages [35]
An AMAP can be viewed as an “annotated image” in which each pixel is a 5-element feature vector: 4 features are associated to four orientations of the pen trajectory in the area around the pixel, and the fifth one is associated to local curvature in the area around the pixel.

[P23-S01115] PDF pages [35]
A particularly useful feature of the AMAP representation is that it makes very few assumptions about the nature of the input trajectory.

[P23-S01116] PDF pages [35]
It does not depend on stroke ordering or writing speed, and it can be used with all types of handwriting (capital, lower case, cursive, punctuation, symbols).

[P23-S01117] PDF pages [35]
Unlike many other representations (such as global features), AMAPs can be computed for complete words without re-quiring segmentation.

[P23-S01118] PDF pages [35]
One of the best networks we found for both online and offline character recognition is a 5-layer convolutional network somewhat similar to LeNet-5 (Figure 2), but with multiple input planes and different numbers of units on the last two layers; layer 1: convolution with 8 kernels of size 3x3, layer 2: 2x2 sub-sampling, layer 3: convolution with 25 kernels of size 5x5, layer 4 convolution with 84 kernels of size 4x4, layer 5: 2x1 sub-sampling, classification layer: 95 RBF units (one per class in the full printable ASCII set).

[P23-S01119] PDF pages [35]
The distributed codes on the output are the same as for LeNet-5, except they are adaptive unlike with LeNet-5.

[P23-S01120] PDF pages [35]
When used in the heuristic over-segmentation system, the input to above network consisted of an AMAP with five planes, 20 rows and 18 columns.

[P23-S01121] PDF pages [35]
It was determined that this resolution was sufficient for representing handwritten characters.

[P23-S01122] PDF pages [35]
In the SDNN version, the number of columns was varied according to the width of the input word.

[P23-S01123] PDF pages [35]
Once the number of sub-sampling layers and the sizes of the kernels are chosen, the sizes of all the layers, including the input, are determined unambiguously.

[P23-S01124] PDF pages [35]
The only architectural parameters that remain to be selected are the number of feature maps in each layer, and the information as to what feature map is connected to what other feature map.

[P23-S01125] PDF pages [35, 36]
In our case, the sub-sampling rates were chosen as small as possible (2x2), and the kernels as small as pos- sible in the first layer (3x3) to limit the total number of connections.

[P23-S01126] PDF pages [36]
Kernel sizes in the upper layers are chosen to be as small as possible while satisfying the size constraints mentioned above.

[P23-S01127] PDF pages [36]
Larger architectures did not necessarily perform better and required considerably more time to be trained.

[P23-S01128] PDF pages [36]
A very small architecture with half the input field also performed worse, because of insufficient input resolution.

[P23-S01129] PDF pages [36]
Note that the input resolution is nonetheless much less than for optical character recognition, because the angle and curvature provide more information than would a single grey level at each pixel.

[P23-S01130] PDF pages [36]
First, we kept the centers of the RBFs fixed, and trained the network weights so as to minimize the output distance of the RBF unit corresponding to the correct class.

[P23-S01131] PDF pages [36]
This is equivalent to minimizing the mean-squared error between the previous layer and the center of the correct-class RBF.

[P23-S01132] PDF pages [36]
This bootstrap phase was performed on isolated characters.

[P23-S01133] PDF pages [36]
In the second phase, all the parameters, network weights and RBF centers were trained globally to minimize a discriminative criterion at the word level.

[P23-S01134] PDF pages [36]
With the Heuristic Over-Segmentation approach, the GTN was composed of four main Graph Transformers: 1.

[P23-S01135] PDF pages [36]
The Segmentation Transformer performs the Heuristic Over-Segmentation, and outputs the segmentation graph.

[P23-S01136] PDF pages [36]
An AMAP is then computed for each image attached to the arcs of this graph.

[P23-S01137] PDF pages [36]
The Character Recognition Transformer applies the the convolutional network character recognizer to each candidate segment, and outputs the recognition graph, with penalties and classes on each arc.

[P23-S01138] PDF pages [36]
The Composition Transformer composes the recognition graph with a grammar graph representing a language model incorporating lexical constraints.

[P23-S01139] PDF pages [36]
The Beam Search Transformer extracts a good interpretation from the interpretation graph.

[P23-S01140] PDF pages [36]
This task could have been achieved with the usual Viterbi Transformer.

[P23-S01141] PDF pages [36]
The Beam Search algorithm however implements pruning strategies which are appropriate for large interpretation graphs.

[P23-S01142] PDF pages [36]
With the SDNN approach, the main Graph Transformers are the following: 1.

[P23-S01143] PDF pages [36]
The SDNN Transformer replicates the convolutional network over the a whole word image, and outputs a recognition graph that is a linear graph with class penalties for every window centered at regular intervals on the input image.

[P23-S01144] PDF pages [36]
The Character-Level Composition Transformer composes the recognition graph with a left-to-right HMM for each character class (as in Figure 27).

[P23-S01145] PDF pages [36]
The Word-Level Composition Transformer composes the output of the previous transformer with a language model incorporating lexical constraints, and outputs the interpretation graph.

[P23-S01146] PDF pages [36]
The Beam Search Transformer extracts a good in-terpretation from the interpretation graph.

[P23-S01147] PDF pages [36]
In this application, the language model simply constrains the final output graph to represent sequences of character labels from a given dictionary.

[P23-S01148] PDF pages [36]
Furthermore, the interpretation graph is not actually completely instantiated: the only nodes created are those that are needed by the Beam Search module.

[P23-S01149] PDF pages [36]
The interpretation graph is therefore represented procedurally rather than explicitly.

[P23-S01150] PDF pages [36]
A crucial contribution of this research was the joint training of all graph transformer modules within the network with respect to a single criterion, as explained in Sections VI and VIII.

[P23-S01151] PDF pages [36]
We used the Discriminative Forward loss function on the final output graph: minimize the forward penalty of the constrained interpretation (i.e., along all the “correct” paths) while maximizing the forward penalty of the whole interpretation graph (i.e., along all the paths).

[P23-S01152] PDF pages [36]
During global training, the loss function was optimized with the stochastic diagonal Levenberg-Marquardt procedure described in Appendix C, that uses second derivatives to compute optimal learning rates.

[P23-S01153] PDF pages [36]
This optimization operates on all the parameters in the system, most notably the network weights and the RBF centers.

[P23-S01154] PDF pages [36]
In the first set of experiments, we evaluated the generalization ability of the neural network classifier coupled with the word normalization preprocessing and AMAP input representation.

[P23-S01155] PDF pages [36]
All results are in writer independent mode (different writers in training and testing).

[P23-S01156] PDF pages [36]
Initial training on isolated characters was performed on a database of approximately 100,000 hand printed characters (95 classes of upper case, lower case, digits, and punctuation).

[P23-S01157] PDF pages [36]
Tests on a database of isolated characters were performed separately on the four types of characters: upper case (2.99% error On 9122 patterns), lower case (4.15% error on 8201 patterns), digits (1.4% error on 2938 patterns), and punctuation (4.3% error on 881 patterns).

[P23-S01158] PDF pages [36]
Experiments were performed with the network architecture described above.

[P23-S01159] PDF pages [36]
To enhance the robustness of the recognizer to variations in position, size, orientation, and other distortions, additional training data was generated by applying local affine transformations to the original characters.

[P23-S01160] PDF pages [36]
The second and third set of experiments concerned the recognition of lower case words (writer independent).

[P23-S01161] PDF pages [36]
The tests were performed on a database of 881 words.

[P23-S01162] PDF pages [36]
First we evaluated the improvements brought by the word normalization to the system.

[P23-S01163] PDF pages [36]
For the SDNN/HMM system we have to use word-level normalization since the network sees one whole word at a time.

[P23-S01164] PDF pages [36]
With the Heuristic Over-Segmentation system, and before doing any word-level training, we obtained with character-level normalization 7.3% and 3.5% word and character errors (adding in-sertions, deletions and substitutions) when the search was constrained within a 25461-word dictionary.

[P23-S01165] PDF pages [36]
When using the word normalization preprocessing instead of a character level normalization, error rates dropped to 4.6% and 2.0% for word and character errors respectively, i.e., a relative drop of 37% and 43% in word and character error respectively.

[P23-S01166] PDF pages [36, 37]
This suggests that normalizing the word in its entirety is better than first segmenting it and then normalizing and processing each of the segments.

[P23-S01167] PDF pages [37]
Comparative results (character error rates) showing the improvement brought by global training on the SDNN/HMM hybrid, and on the Heuristic Over-Segmentation system (HOS), without and with a 25461 words dictionary.

[P23-S01168] PDF pages [37]
In the third set of experiments, we measured the improvements obtained with the joint training of the neural network and the post-processor with the word-level criterion, in comparison to training based only on the errors performed at the character level.

[P23-S01169] PDF pages [37]
After initial training on individual characters as above, global word-level discriminative training was performed with a database of 3500 lower case words.

[P23-S01170] PDF pages [37]
For the SDNN/HMM system, without any dictionary constraints, the error rates dropped from 38% and 12.4% word and character error to 26% and 8.2% respectively after word-level training, i.e., a relative drop of 32% and 34%.

[P23-S01171] PDF pages [37]
For the Heuristic Over-Segmentation system and a slightly improved architecture, without any dictionary constraints, the error rates dropped from 22.5% and 8.5% word and character error to 17% and 6.3% re-spectively, i.e., a relative drop of 24.4% and 25.6%.

[P23-S01172] PDF pages [37]
With a 25461-word dictionary, errors dropped from 4.6% and 2.0% word and character errors to 3.2% and 1.4% respectively after word-level training, i.e., a relative drop of 30.4% and 30.0%.

[P23-S01173] PDF pages [37]
Even lower error rates can be obtained by drastically reducing the size of the dictionary to 350 words, yielding 1.6% and 0.94% word and character errors.

[P23-S01174] PDF pages [37]
These results clearly demonstrate the usefulness of globally trained Neural-Net/HMM hybrids for handwriting recognition.

[P23-S01175] PDF pages [37]
This confirms similar results obtained earlier in speech recognition [77].

[P23-S01176] PDF pages [37]
This section describes a GTN based Check Reading System, intended for immediate industrial deployment.

[P23-S01177] PDF pages [37]
It also shows how the use of Gradient Based-Learning and GTNs make this deployment fast and cost-effective while yielding an accurate and reliable solution.

[P23-S01178] PDF pages [37]
The verification of the amount on a check is a task that is extremely time and money consuming for banks.

[P23-S01179] PDF pages [37]
As a consequence, there is a very high interest in automating the process as much as possible (see for example [112], [113], [114]).

[P23-S01180] PDF pages [37]
Even a partial automation would result in considerable cost reductions.

[P23-S01181] PDF pages [37]
The threshold of economic viability for automatic check readers, as set by the bank, is when 50% of the checks are read with less than 1% error.

[P23-S01182] PDF pages [37]
The other 50% of the check being rejected and sent to human operators.

[P23-S01183] PDF pages [37]
In such a case, we describe the performance of the system as 50% correct / 49% reject / 1% error.

[P23-S01184] PDF pages [37]
The system presented here was one of the first to cross that threshold on representative mixtures of business and personal checks.

[P23-S01185] PDF pages [37]
Checks contain at least two versions of the amount.

[P23-S01186] PDF pages [37]
The Courtesy amount is written with numerals, while the Legal amount is written with letters.

[P23-S01187] PDF pages [37]
On business checks, which are generally machine-printed, these amounts are relatively easy to read, but quite difficult to find due to the lack of standard for business check layout.

[P23-S01188] PDF pages [37]
On the other hand, these amounts on personal checks are easy to find but much harder to read.

[P23-S01189] PDF pages [37]
For simplicity (and speed requirements), our initial task is to read the Courtesy amount only.

[P23-S01190] PDF pages [37]
This task consists of two main steps: e The system has to find, among all the fields (lines of text), the candidates that are the most likely to contain the courtesy amount.

[P23-S01191] PDF pages [37]
This is obvious for many personal checks, where the position of the amount is standardized.

[P23-S01192] PDF pages [37]
However, as already noted, finding the amount can be rather difficult in business checks, even for the human eye.

[P23-S01193] PDF pages [37]
There are many Strings of digits, such as the check number, the date, or even “not to exceed” amounts, that can be confused with the actual amount.

[P23-S01194] PDF pages [37]
In many cases, it is very difficult to decide which candidate is the courtesy amount before performing a full recognition.

[P23-S01195] PDF pages [37]
The GTN methodology was used to build a check amount reading system that handles both personal checks and business checks.

[P23-S01196] PDF pages [37]
A GTN for Check Amount Recognition We now describe the successive graph transformations that allow this network to read the check amount (cf.

[P23-S01197] PDF pages [37]
Each Graph Transformer produces a graph whose paths encode and score the current hypotheses considered at this stage of the system.

[P23-S01198] PDF pages [37]
The input to the system is a trivial graph with a single arc that carries the image of the whole check (cf.

[P23-S01199] PDF pages [37]
Ty;erq produces an output graph, called the field graph (cf.

[P23-S01200] PDF pages [37]
The penalty term is close to zero if the features suggest that the field is a likely candidate, and is large if the field is deemed less likely to be an amount.

[P23-S01201] PDF pages [38]
A complete check amount reader implemented as a single cascade of Graph Transformer modules.

[P23-S01202] PDF pages [38]
Successive graph transformations progressively extract higher level information.

[P23-S01203] PDF pages [38]
An arc may represent separate dollar and cent amounts as a sequence of fields.

[P23-S01204] PDF pages [38]
In fact, in handwritten checks, the cent amount may be written over a fractional bar, and not aligned at all with the dollar amount.

[P23-S01205] PDF pages [38]
In the worst case, one may find several cent amount candidates (above and below the fraction bar) for the same dollar amount.

[P23-S01206] PDF pages [38]
The segmentation transformer T;.,, similar to the one described in Section VIII examines each zone contained in the field graph, and cuts each image into pieces of ink using heuristic image processing techniques.

[P23-S01207] PDF pages [38]
Each piece of ink may be a whole character or a piece of character.

[P23-S01208] PDF pages [38]
Each arc in the field graph is replaced by its corresponding segmentation graph that represents all possible groupings of pieces of ink.

[P23-S01209] PDF pages [38]
Each field segmentation graph is appended to an arc that contains the penalty of the field in the field graph.

[P23-S01210] PDF pages [38]
Each arc carries the segment image, to-gether with a penalty that provides a first evaluation of the likelihood that the segment actually contains a character.

[P23-S01211] PDF pages [38]
This penalty is obtained with a differentiable function that combines a few simple features such as the space between the pieces of ink or the compliance of the segment image with a global baseline, and a few tunable parameters.

[P23-S01212] PDF pages [38]
The segmentation graph represents all the possible segmentations of all the field images.

[P23-S01213] PDF pages [38]
We can compute the penalty for one segmented field by adding the arc penalties along the corresponding path.

[P23-S01214] PDF pages [38]
As before using a differentiable function for computing the penalties will ensure that the parameters can be optimized globally.

[P23-S01215] PDF pages [38]
The segmenter uses a variety of heuristics to find candidate cut.

[P23-S01216] PDF pages [38]
One of the most important ones is called “hit and deflect” [115].

[P23-S01217] PDF pages [38]
The idea is to cast lines downward from the top of the field image.

[P23-S01218] PDF pages [38]
When a line hits a black pixel, it is deflected so as to follow the contour of the object.

[P23-S01219] PDF pages [38]
When a line hits a local minimum of the upper profile, i.e. when it cannot continue downward without crossing a black pixel, it is just propagated vertically downward through the ink.

[P23-S01220] PDF pages [38]
When two such lines meet each other, they are merged into a single cut.

[P23-S01221] PDF pages [38]
The procedure can be repeated from the bottom up.

[P23-S01222] PDF pages [38]
This strategy allows the separation of touching characters such as double zeros.

[P23-S01223] PDF pages [38]
In our case, the recognizer is LeNet-5, the Convolutional Neural Network described in Section II, whose weights constitute the largest and most important subset of tunable parameters.

[P23-S01224] PDF pages [38]
The recognizer classifies segment images into one of 95 classes (full printable ASCII set) plus a rubbish class for unknown symbols or badly-formed characters.

[P23-S01225] PDF pages [38]
Each arc in the input graph T,,, is replaced by 96 arcs in the output graph.

[P23-S01226] PDF pages [38]
Each of those 96 arcs contains the label of one of the classes, and a penalty that is the sum of the penalty of the corresponding arc in the input (segmentation) graph and the penalty associated with classifying the image in the corresponding class, as computed by the recognizer.

[P23-S01227] PDF pages [38]
In other words, the recognition graph represents a weighted trellis of scored character classes.

[P23-S01228] PDF pages [38]
Each path in this graph represents a possible character string for the corresponding field.

[P23-S01229] PDF pages [38]
We can compute a penalty for this interpretation by adding the penalties along the path.

[P23-S01230] PDF pages [38]
This sequence of characters may or may not be a valid check amount.

[P23-S01231] PDF pages [38]
The composition transformer T,,,,, selects the paths of the recognition graph that represent valid character sequences for check amounts.

[P23-S01232] PDF pages [38]
This transformer takes two graphs as input: the recognition graph, and the grammar graph.

[P23-S01233] PDF pages [38]
The grammar graph contains all possible sequences of symbols that constitute a well-formed amount.

[P23-S01234] PDF pages [38]
The output of the composition transformer, called the in-terpretation graph, contains all the paths in the recognition graph that are compatible with the grammar.

[P23-S01235] PDF pages [38]
The operation that combines the two input graphs to produce the output is a generalized transduction (see Section VIII).A differentiable function is used to compute the data attached to the output arc from the data attached to the input arcs.

[P23-S01236] PDF pages [38]
In our case, the output arc receives the class label of the two arcs, and a penalty computed by simply summing the penalties of the two input arcs (the recognizer penalty, and the arc penalty in the grammar graph).

[P23-S01237] PDF pages [38]
Each path in the interpretation graph represents one interpretation of one segmentation of one field on the check.

[P23-S01238] PDF pages [38]
The sum of the penalties along the path represents the “badness” of the corresponding interpretation and combines evidence from each of the modules along the process, as well as from the grammar.

[P23-S01239] PDF pages [39]
Additional processing required to compute the confidence.

[P23-S01240] PDF pages [39]
Each stage of this check reading system contains tunable parameters.

[P23-S01241] PDF pages [39]
While some of these parameters could be manually adjusted, for example the parameters of the field locator and segmenter, the vast majority of them must be learned, particularly the weights of the neural net recognizer.

[P23-S01242] PDF pages [39]
Prior to globally optimizing the system, each module parameters are initialized with reasonable values.

[P23-S01243] PDF pages [39]
The parameters of the field locator and the segmenter are initialized by hand, while the parameters of the neural net character recognizer are initialized by training on a database of pre-segmented and labeled characters.

[P23-S01244] PDF pages [39]
Then, the entire system is trained globally from whole check images labeled with the correct amount.

[P23-S01245] PDF pages [39]
No explicit segmentation of the amounts is needed to train the system: it is trained at the check level.

[P23-S01246] PDF pages [39]
The loss function E minimized by our global training procedure is the Discriminative Forward criterion described in Section VI: the difference between (a) the forward penalty of the constrained interpretation graph (constrained by the correct label sequence), and (b) the forward penalty of the unconstrained interpretation graph.

[P23-S01247] PDF pages [39]
Derivatives can be back-propagated through the entire structure, although it only practical to do it down to the segmenter.

[P23-S01248] PDF pages [39]
In order to be able to reject checks which are the most likely to carry erroneous Viterbi answers, we must rate them with a confidence, and reject the check if this confidence is below a given threshold.

[P23-S01249] PDF pages [39]
To compare the unnormalized Viterbi Penalties of two different checks would be meaningless when it comes to decide which answer we trust the most.

[P23-S01250] PDF pages [39]
The optimal measure of confidence is the probability of the Viterbi answer given the input image.

[P23-S01251] PDF pages [39]
As seen in Section VI-E, given a target sequence (which, in this case, would be the Viterbi answer), the discriminative forward loss function is an estimate of the logarithm of this probability.

[P23-S01252] PDF pages [39]
Therefore, a simple solution to obtain a good estimate of the confidence is to reuse the interpretation graph (see Figure 33) to compute the discriminative forward loss as described in Figure 21, using as our desired sequence the Viterbi answer.

[P23-S01253] PDF pages [39]
A version of the above system was fully implemented and tested on machine-print business checks.

[P23-S01254] PDF pages [39]
This system is basically a generic GTN engine with task specific heuristics encapsulated in the check and fprop method.

[P23-S01255] PDF pages [39]
As a consequence, the amount of code to write was minimal: mostly the adaptation of an earlier segmenter into the segmentation transformer.

[P23-S01256] PDF pages [39]
The system that deals with hand-written or personal checks was based on earlier implementations that used the GTN concept in a restricted way.

[P23-S01257] PDF pages [39]
The neural network classifier was initially trained on 500,000 images of character images from various origins spanning the entire printable ASCII set.

[P23-S01258] PDF pages [39]
This contained both handwritten and machine-printed characters that had been previously size normalized at the string level.

[P23-S01259] PDF pages [39]
Additional images were generated by randomly distorting the original images using simple affine transformations of the images.

[P23-S01260] PDF pages [39]
The network was then further trained on character images that had been automatically segmented from check images and manually truthed.

[P23-S01261] PDF pages [39]
The network was also initially trained to reject non-characters that resulted from segmentation errors.

[P23-S01262] PDF pages [39]
The recognizer was then inserted in the check reading system and a small subset of the parameters were trained globally (at the field level) on whole check images.

[P23-S01263] PDF pages [39]
On 646 business checks that were automatically categorized as machine printed the performance was 82% correctly recognized checks, 1% errors, and 17% rejects.

[P23-S01264] PDF pages [39]
This can be compared to the performance of the previous system on the same test set: 68% correct, 1% errors, and 31% rejects.

[P23-S01265] PDF pages [39]
A check is categorized as machine-printed when characters that are near a standard position Dollar sign are detected as machine printed, or when, if nothing is found in the standard position, at least one courtesy amount candidate is found somewhere else.

[P23-S01266] PDF pages [39]
The improvement is attributed to three main causes.

[P23-S01267] PDF pages [39]
First the neural network recognizer was bigger, and trained on more data.

[P23-S01268] PDF pages [39]
Second, because of the GTN architecture, the new system could take advantage of grammatical constraints in a much more efficient way than the previous system.

[P23-S01269] PDF pages [39]
Third, the GTN architecture provided extreme flexibility for testing heuristics, adjusting parameters, and tuning the system.

[P23-S01270] PDF pages [39]
This last point is more important than it seems.

[P23-S01271] PDF pages [39]
The GTN framework separates the “algorithmic” part of the system from the “knowledge-based” part of the system, allowing easy adjustments of the latter.

[P23-S01272] PDF pages [39]
The importance of global training was only minor in this task because the global training only concerned a small subset of the parameters.

[P23-S01273] PDF pages [39]
An independent test performed by systems integrators in 1995 showed the superiority of this system over other commercial Courtesy amount reading systems.

[P23-S01274] PDF pages [39]
The system was integrated in NCR's line of check reading systems.

[P23-S01275] PDF pages [39, 40]
It has been fielded in several banks across the US since June 1996, and has been reading millions of checks per day since then.

[P23-S01276] PDF pages [40]
During the short history of automatic pattern recognition, increasing the role of learning seems to have invariably improved the overall performance of recognition systems.

[P23-S01277] PDF pages [40]
The systems described in this paper are more evidence to this fact.

[P23-S01278] PDF pages [40]
Convolutional Neural Networks have been shown to eliminate the need for hand-crafted feature extractors.

[P23-S01279] PDF pages [40]
Graph Transformer Networks have been shown to reduce the need for hand-crafted heuristics, manual labeling, and manual parameter tuning in document recognition systems.

[P23-S01280] PDF pages [40]
As training data becomes plentiful, as computers get faster, as our understanding of learning algorithms improves, recognition systems will rely more and more of learning, and their performance will improve.

[P23-S01281] PDF pages [40]
Just as the back-propagation algorithm elegantly solved the credit assignment problem in multi-layer neural networks, the gradient-based learning procedure for Graph Transformer Networks introduced in this paper solves the credit assignment problem in systems whose functional architecture dynamically changes with each new input.

[P23-S01282] PDF pages [40]
The learning algorithms presented here are in a sense nothing more than unusual forms of gradient descent in complex, dynamic architectures, with efficient back-propagation algorithms to compute the gradient.

[P23-S01283] PDF pages [40]
The results in this paper help establish the usefulness and relevance of gradientbased minimization methods as a general organizing principle for learning in large systems.

[P23-S01284] PDF pages [40]
It was shown that all the steps of a document analysis system can be formulated as graph transformers through which gradients can be back-propagated.

[P23-S01285] PDF pages [40]
Even in the non-trainable parts of the system, the design philosophy in terms of graph transformation provides a clear separation between domain-specific heuristics (e.g. segmentation heuristics) and generic, procedural knowledge (the generalized transduction algorithm) It is worth pointing out that data generating models (such as HMMs) and the Mazimum Likelihood Principle were not called upon to justify most of the architectures and the training criteria described in this paper.

[P23-S01286] PDF pages [40]
Gradient based learning applied to global discriminative loss functions guarantees optimal classification and rejection without the use of “hard to justify” principles that put strong constraints on the system architecture, often at the expense of performances.

[P23-S01287] PDF pages [40]
More specifically, the methods and architectures pre-sented in this paper offer generic solutions to a large number of problems encountered in pattern recognition systems: 1.

[P23-S01288] PDF pages [40]
Feature extraction is traditionally a fixed transform, generally derived from some expert prior knowledge about the task.

[P23-S01289] PDF pages [40]
This relies on the probably incorrect assumption that the human designer is able to capture all the relevant information in the input.

[P23-S01290] PDF pages [40]
We have shown that the application of Gradient-Based Learning to Convolutional Neural Networks allows to learn appropriate features from examples.

[P23-S01291] PDF pages [40]
The success of this approach was demonstrated in extensive comparative digit recognition experiments on the NIST database.

[P23-S01292] PDF pages [40]
Segmentation and recognition of objects in images cannot be completely decoupled.

[P23-S01293] PDF pages [40]
Instead of taking hard segmentation decisions too early, we have used Heuristic Over- Segmentation to generate and evaluate a large number of hypotheses in parallel, postponing any decision until the overall criterion is minimized.

[P23-S01294] PDF pages [40]
Hand truthing images to obtain segmented characters for training a character recognizer is expensive and does not take into account the way in which a whole document or sequence of characters will be recognized (in particular the fact that some segmentation candidates may be wrong, even though they may look like true characters).

[P23-S01295] PDF pages [40]
Instead we train multi-module systems to optimize a global measure of performance, which does not require time consuming detailed hand-truthing, and yields significantly better recognition performance, because it allows to train these modules to cooperate towards a common goal.

[P23-S01296] PDF pages [40]
Ambiguities inherent in the segmentation, character recognition, and linguistic model should be integrated optimally.

[P23-S01297] PDF pages [40]
Instead of using a sequence of task-dependent heuristics to combine these sources of information, we have proposed a unified framework in which generalized transduction methods are applied to graphs representing a weighted set of hypotheses about the input.

[P23-S01298] PDF pages [40]
The success of this approach was demonstrated with a commercially deployed check reading system that reads millions of business and personal checks per day: the generalized transduction engine resides in only a few hundred lines of code.

[P23-S01299] PDF pages [40]
Traditional recognition systems rely on many handcrafted heuristics to isolate individually recognizable objects.

[P23-S01300] PDF pages [40]
The promising Space Displacement Neural Network approach draws on the robustness and efficiency of Convolutional Neural Networks to avoid explicit segmentation altogether.

[P23-S01301] PDF pages [40]
Simultaneous automatic learning of segmentation and recognition can be achieved with Gradient-Based Learning methods.

[P23-S01302] PDF pages [40]
This paper presents a small number of examples of graph transformer modules, but it is clear that the concept can be applied to many situations where the domain knowledge or the state information can be represented by graphs.

[P23-S01303] PDF pages [40]
This is the case in many audio signal recognition tasks, and visual scene analysis applications.

[P23-S01304] PDF pages [40]
Future work will attempt to apply Graph Transformer Networks to such problems, with the hope of allowing more reliance on automatic learning, and less on detailed engineering.

[P23-S01305] PDF pages [40]
APPENDICES A. Pre-conditions for faster convergence As seen before, the squashing function used in our Convolutional Networks is f(a) = Atanh(Sa).

[P23-S01306] PDF pages [40]
Symmetric functions are believed to yield faster convergence, although the learning can become extremely slow if the weights are too small.

[P23-S01307] PDF pages [40, 41]
The cause of this problem is that in weight space the origin is a fixed point of the learning dynamics, and, although it is a saddle point, it is attractive in almost all directions [116].

[P23-S01308] PDF pages [41]
With this choice of parameters, the equalities f(1) = 1 and f(-1) = -1 are satisfied.

[P23-S01309] PDF pages [41]
The rationale behind this is that the overall gain of the squashing transformation is around 1 in normal operating conditions, and the interpretation of the state of the network is simplified.

[P23-S01310] PDF pages [41]
Moreover, the absolute value of the second derivative of f is a maximum at +1 and -1, which improves the convergence towards the end of the learning session.

[P23-S01311] PDF pages [41]
This particular choice of parameters is merely a convenience, and does not affect the result.

[P23-S01312] PDF pages [41]
Before training, the weights are initialized with random values using a uniform distribution between -2.4/F; and 2.4/F; where F; is the number of inputs (fan-in) of the unit which the connection belongs to.

[P23-S01313] PDF pages [41]
Since several connections share a weight, this rule could be difficult to apply, but in our case, all connections sharing a same weight belong to units with identical fan-ins.

[P23-S01314] PDF pages [41]
The reason for dividing by the fan-in is that we would like the initial standard deviation of the weighted sums to be in the same range for each unit, and to fall within the normal operating region of the sigmoid.

[P23-S01315] PDF pages [41]
If the initial weights are too small, the gradients are very small and the learning is slow.

[P23-S01316] PDF pages [41]
If they are too large, the sigmoids are saturated and the gradient is also very small.

[P23-S01317] PDF pages [41]
The standard deviation of the weighted sum scales like the square root of the number of inputs when the inputs are independent, and it scales linearly with the number of inputs if the inputs are highly correlated.

[P23-S01318] PDF pages [41]
We chose to assume the second hypothesis since some units receive highly correlated signals.

[P23-S01319] PDF pages [41]
B. Stochastic Gradient vs Batch Gradient Gradient-Based Learning algorithms can use one of two classes of methods to update the parameters.

[P23-S01320] PDF pages [41]
The first method, dubbed “Batch Gradient”, is the classical one: the gradients are accumulated over the entire training set, and the parameters are updated after the exact gradient has been so computed.

[P23-S01321] PDF pages [41]
In the second method, called “Stochastic Gradient”, a partial, or noisy, gradient is evaluated on the basis of one single training sample (or a small number of samples), and the parameters are updated using this approximate gradient.

[P23-S01322] PDF pages [41]
The training samples can be selected randomly or according to a properly randomized sequence.

[P23-S01323] PDF pages [41]
In the stochastic version, the gradient estimates are noisy, but the parameters are updated much more often than with the batch version.

[P23-S01324] PDF pages [41]
An empirical result of considerable practical importance is that on tasks with large, redundant data sets, the stochastic version is considerably faster than the batch version, sometimes by orders of magnitude [117].

[P23-S01325] PDF pages [41]
Although the reasons for this are not totally understood theoretically, an intuitive explanation can be found in the following extreme example.

[P23-S01326] PDF pages [41]
Let us take an example where the training database is composed of two copies of the same subset.

[P23-S01327] PDF pages [41]
Then accumulating the gradient over the whole set would cause redundant computations to be performed.

[P23-S01328] PDF pages [41]
This idea can be generalized to training sets where there exist no precise repetition of the same pattern but where some redundancy is present.

[P23-S01329] PDF pages [41]
In fact stochastic update must be better when there is redundancy, i.e., when a certain level of generalization is expected.

[P23-S01330] PDF pages [41]
Many authors have claimed that second-order methods should be used in lieu of gradient descent for neural net training.

[P23-S01331] PDF pages [41]
The literature abounds with recommendations [118] for classical second-order methods such as the Gauss-Newton or Levenberg-Marquardt algorithms, for Quasi-Newton methods such as the Broyden-Fletcher- Goldfarb-Shanno method (BFGS), Limited-storage BFGS, or for various versions of the Conjugate Gradients (CG) method.

[P23-S01332] PDF pages [41]
Unfortunately, all of the above methods are unsuitable for training large neural networks on large data sets.

[P23-S01333] PDF pages [41]
The Gauss-Newton and Levenberg-Marquardt methods require O(N*) operations per update, where N is the number of parameters, which makes them impractical for even moderate size networks.

[P23-S01334] PDF pages [41]
Limited- Storage BFGS and Conjugate Gradient require only O(N) operations per update so they would appear appropriate.

[P23-S01335] PDF pages [41]
Unfortunately, their convergence speed relies on an accurate evaluation of successive “conjugate descent directions” which only makes sense in “batch” mode.

[P23-S01336] PDF pages [41]
For large data sets, the speed-up brought by these methods over regular batch gradient descent cannot match the enormous speed up brought by the use of stochastic gradient.

[P23-S01337] PDF pages [41]
Several authors have attempted to use Conjugate Gradient with small batches, or batches of increasing sizes [119], [120], but those attempts have not yet been demonstrated to surpass a carefully tuned stochastic gradient.

[P23-S01338] PDF pages [41]
Our experiments were performed with a stochastic method that scales the parameter axes so as to minimize the eccentricity of the error surface.

[P23-S01339] PDF pages [41]
Owing to the reasons given in Appendix B, we prefer to update the weights after each presentation of a single pattern in accordance with stochastic update methods.

[P23-S01340] PDF pages [41]
The patterns are presented in a constant random order, and the training set is typically repeated 20 times.

[P23-S01341] PDF pages [41]
Our update algorithm is dubbed the Stochastic Diagonal Levenberg-Marquardt method where an individual learning rate (step size) is computed for each parameter (weight) before each pass through the training set [20], [121], [34].

[P23-S01342] PDF pages [41]
These learning rates are computed using the diagonal terms of an estimate of the Gauss-Newton approximation to the Hessian (second derivative) matrix.

[P23-S01343] PDF pages [41]
This algorithm is not believed to bring a tremendous increase in learning speed but it converges reliably without requiring extensive adjustments of the learning parameters.

[P23-S01344] PDF pages [41]
It corrects major illconditioning of the loss function that are due to the peculiarities of the network architecture and the training data.

[P23-S01345] PDF pages [41]
The additional cost of using this procedure over standard stochastic gradient descent is negligible.

[P23-S01346] PDF pages [42]
As stated previously, the step sizes e, are not constant but are function of the second derivative of the loss function along the axis wz: u] “* M+ her (1) where y is a hand-picked constant and hx; is an estimate of the second derivative of the loss function E with re-spect to w,.

[P23-S01347] PDF pages [42]
The larger hx, the smaller the weight update.

[P23-S01348] PDF pages [42]
The parameter yz prevents the step size from becoming too large when the second derivative is small, very much like the “model-trust” methods, and the Levenberg-Marquardt methods in non-linear optimization [8].

[P23-S01349] PDF pages [42]
The first approximation is to drop the off-diagonal terms of the Hessian with respect to the connection weights in the above equa- tion: » E Rep = .

[P23-S01350] PDF pages [42]
Interestingly, there is an efficient algorithm to compute those second derivatives which is very similar to the back-propagation procedure used to compute the first derivatives [20], [121]: OPEP i, 5 9 OEP OEP da?

[P23-S01351] PDF pages [42]
Unfortunately, using those derivatives leads to well-known problems associated with every Newton-like algorithm: these terms can be negative, and can cause the gradient algorithm to move uphill instead of downhill.

[P23-S01352] PDF pages [42]
Therefore, our second approximation is a well-known trick, called the Gauss-Newton approximation, which guarantees that the second derivative estimates are non-negative.

[P23-S01353] PDF pages [42]
The Gauss- Newton approximation essentially ignores the non-linearity of the estimated function (the Neural Network in our case), but not that of the loss function.

[P23-S01354] PDF pages [42]
Oa This is very similar to the formula for back-propagating the first derivatives, except that the sigmoid's derivative and the weight values are squared.

[P23-S01355] PDF pages [42]
The right-hand side is a sum of products of non-negative terms, therefore the left-hand side term is non-negative.

[P23-S01356] PDF pages [42]
The third approximation we make is that we do not run the average in Equation 24 over the entire training set, but run it on a small subset of the training set instead.

[P23-S01357] PDF pages [42]
In addition the re-estimation does not need to be done of-ten since the second order properties of the error surface change rather slowly.

[P23-S01358] PDF pages [42]
In the experiments described in this paper, we re-estimate the h,, on 500 patterns before each training pass through the training set.

[P23-S01359] PDF pages [42]
Since the size of the training set is 60,000, the additional cost of re-estimating the hy, is negligible.

[P23-S01360] PDF pages [42]
The estimates are not particularly sensitive to the particular subset of the training set used in the averaging.

[P23-S01361] PDF pages [42]
This seems to suggest that the second-order properties of the error surface are mainly determined by the structure of the network, rather than by the detailed statistics of the samples.

[P23-S01362] PDF pages [42]
This algorithm is particularly useful for shared-weight networks because the weight sharing creates ill-conditionning of the error surface.

[P23-S01363] PDF pages [42]
Because of the sharing, one single parameter in the first few layers can have an enormous influence on the output.

[P23-S01364] PDF pages [42]
Consequently, the second derivative of the error with respect to this parameter may be very large, while it can be quite small for other parameters elsewhere in the network.

[P23-S01365] PDF pages [42]
The above algorithm compensates for that phenomenon.

[P23-S01366] PDF pages [42]
Unlike most other second-order acceleration methods for back-propagation, the above method works in stochastic mode.

[P23-S01367] PDF pages [42]
It uses a diagonal approximation of the Hessian.

[P23-S01368] PDF pages [42]
Like the classical Levenberg-Marquardt algorithm, it uses a “safety” factor ps to prevent the step sizes from getting too large if the second derivative estimates are small.

[P23-S01369] PDF pages [42]
Hence the method is called the Stochastic Diagonal Levenberg- Marquardt method.

[P23-S01370] PDF pages [43]
Some of the systems described in this paper is the work of many researchers now at AT&T, and Lucent Technologies.

[P23-S01371] PDF pages [43]
In particular, Christopher Burges, Craig Nohl, Troy Cauble and Jane Bromley contributed much to the check reading system.

[P23-S01372] PDF pages [43]
Experimental results described in section III include contributions by Chris Burges, Aymeric Brunot, Corinna Cortes, Harris Drucker, Larry Jackel, Urs Miiller, Bernhard Schdlkopf, and Patrice Simard.

[P23-S01500] PDF pages [46]
Yann LeCun Yann LeCun received a Dipléme d'Ingénieur from the Ecole Supérieure d'Ingénieur en Electrotechnique et Electronique, Paris in 1983, and a PhD in Computer Science from the Université Pierre et Marie Curie, Paris, in 1987, during which he proposed an early version of the back-propagation learning algorithm for neural networks.

[P23-S01501] PDF pages [46]
He then joined the Department of Computer Science at the University of Toronto as a research associate.

[P23-S01502] PDF pages [46]
In 1988, he joined the Adaptive Systems Research Department at AT&T Bell Laboratories in Holmdel, NJ, where he worked among other thing on neural networks, machine learning, and handwriting recognition.

[P23-S01503] PDF pages [46]
Following AT&T's second breakup in 1996, he became head of the Image Processing Services Research Department at AT&T Labs-Research.

[P23-S01504] PDF pages [46]
He is serving on the board of the Machine Learning Journal, and has served as associate editor of the IEEE Trans.

[P23-S01505] PDF pages [46]
He is general chair of the ” Machines that Learn” workshop held every year since 1986 in Snowbird, Utah.

[P23-S01506] PDF pages [46]
He is a member of the IEEE Neural Network for Signal Processing Technical Committee.

[P23-S01507] PDF pages [46]
He has published over 70 technical papers and book chapters on neural networks, machine learning, pattern recognition, handwriting recognition, document understanding, image processing, VLSI design, and information theory.

[P23-S01508] PDF pages [46]
In addition to the above topics, his current interests include video-based user interfaces, image compression, and content-based indexing of multimedia material.

[P23-S01509] PDF pages [46]
Léon Bottou Léon Bottou received a Diplbme from Ecole Polytechnique, Paris in 1987, a Magistére en Mathématiques Fondamentales et Appliquées et Informatiques from Ecole Normale Supérieure, Paris in 1988, and a PhD in Computer Science from Université de Paris- Sud in 1991, during which he worked on speech recognition and proposed a framework for stochastic gradient learning and global training.

[P23-S01510] PDF pages [46]
He then joined the Adaptive Systems Re-search Department at AT&T Bell Laboratories where he worked on neural network, statistical learning theory and local learning algorithms.

[P23-S01511] PDF pages [46]
He returned to France in 1992 as a research engineer at ONERA.

[P23-S01512] PDF pages [46]
He then became chairman of Neuristique S.A., a company making neural network simulators and traffic forecasting software.

[P23-S01513] PDF pages [46]
He eventually came back to AT&T Bell Laboratories in 1995 where he worked on graph transformer networks for optical character recognition.

[P23-S01514] PDF pages [46]
He is now a member of the Image Processing Services Research Department at AT&T Labs-Research.

[P23-S01515] PDF pages [46]
Besides learning algorithms, his current interests include arithmetic coding, image compression and indexing.

[P23-S01516] PDF pages [46]
Yoshua Bengio Yoshua Bengio received his B.Eng.

[P23-S01517] PDF pages [46]
In 1991-1992 he was a post-doctoral fellow at the Massachusetts Institute of Technology.

[P23-S01518] PDF pages [46]
In 1992 he joined AT&T Bell Laboratories, which later became AT&T Labs-Research.

[P23-S01519] PDF pages [46]
In 1993 he joined the faculty of the computer science department of the Université de Montréal where he is now an associate professor.

[P23-S01520] PDF pages [46]
Since his first work on neural networks in 1986, his research interests have been centered around learning algorithms especially for data with a sequential or spatial nature, such as speech, handwriting, and time-series.

[P23-S01521] PDF pages [46]
Patrick Haffner Patrick Haffner graduated from Ecole Polytechnique, Paris, France in 1987 and from Ecole Nationale Supérieure des Télécommunications (ENST), Paris, France in 1989.

[P23-S01522] PDF pages [46]
He received his Ph.D in speech and signal processing from ENST in 1994.

[P23-S01523] PDF pages [46]
In 1988 and 1990, he worked with Alex Waibel on the design of the TDNN and the MS-TDNN architectures at ATR (Japan) and Carnegie Mellon University.

[P23-S01524] PDF pages [46]
From 1989 to 1995, as a re-search scientist for CNET/France-Télécom in Lannion, France, he developed connectionist learning algorithms for telephone speech recognition.

[P23-S01525] PDF pages [46]
In 1995, he joined AT&T Bell Laboratories and worked on the application of Optical Character Recognition and transducers to the processing of financial documents.

[P23-S01526] PDF pages [46]
In 1997, he joined the Image Processing Services Research Department at AT&T Labs-Research.

[P23-S01527] PDF pages [46]
His research interests include statistical and connectionist models for sequence recognition, machine learning, speech and image recognition, and information theory.
